<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove that a closed Einstein four-manifold with positive Einstein constant, nonnegative sectional curvature, and a zero-curvature plane has universal Riemannian cover isometric to a product of two round two-spheres. Under the normalization $`\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}\nolimits =3g`$, both spheres have radius $`1/\sqrt3`$. The proof extends coupled estimates for the two Weyl curvature blocks to the boundary of the sectional-curvature cone and determines their equality case.

- **C01** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): Let $(M^4,g)$ be a connected smooth closed Riemannian manifold satisfying $$\operatorname{Ric}_g=3g,\qquad K_g(\sigma)\ge0$$ for every tangent two-plane $\sigma$. If $K_g(\sigma_0)=0$ for at least one tangent two-plane, then the universal Riemannian cover is isometric to $$S^2(1/\sqrt3)\times S^2(1/\sqrt3)$$ with its product metric. In particular, the tangent bundle of the universal cover is the orthogonal sum of two parallel rank-two subbundles.
  - Explanation: A zero sectional-curvature plane forces a round product on the universal Riemannian cover under the stated Einstein and compactness hypotheses.
- **C02** (classification, [source](#stmt-cor-nonnegative-classification)): Let $(M^4,g)$ be connected, smooth, and closed, with $\operatorname{Ric}=3g$ and nonnegative sectional curvature. Its universal Riemannian cover is one of the round $S^4$ of curvature $1$, the complex projective plane with its Fubini–Study metric normalized by $\operatorname{Ric}=3g$, or $S^2(1/\sqrt3)\times S^2(1/\sqrt3)$.
  - Explanation: The universal cover belongs to the stated list of symmetric Riemannian geometries.
- **C03** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-curvature-blocks)): There are symmetric trace-free endomorphisms $T\in\operatorname{Sym}_0(\Lambda^+)$ and $U\in\operatorname{Sym}_0(\Lambda^-)$ such that the curvature operator is $$\mathcal R_{\Lambda^2} =\begin{pmatrix}\operatorname{Id}+T&0\\ 0&\operatorname{Id}+U\end{pmatrix}.$$ At a point where these endomorphisms are diagonal, with eigenvalues $t_s,u_s$, the curvature tensor is $$R_{ikjl} =\frac12\sum_s(1+t_s)I_{s,ik}I_{s,jl} +\frac12\sum_s(1+u_s)J_{s,ik}J_{s,jl}.$$ Put $$x=-\min_s t_s,\qquad y=-\min_s u_s,\qquad v=\frac{\|T\|}{\sqrt6},\qquad b=\frac{\|U\|}{\sqrt6},$$ where the matrix norms are Hilbert–Schmidt norms. Then $$\sec_g\ge0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad x+y\le2, \qquad v\le x\le2v,\qquad b\le y\le2b.$$ Both Weyl blocks, viewed as curvature four-tensors, have zero divergence. In particular, for $Z=\nabla T$, whose matrix entries are one-forms, the differential Bianchi constraint is $$\sum_s I_s Z_{sr}=0\qquad(r=1,2,3).$$
  - Explanation: The least Weyl eigenvalues characterize nonnegative sectional curvature and control the normalized block norms.
- **C04** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-spin-algebra)): A spin-$j$ representation has weights $-j,-j+1,\ldots,j$ for $\Sigma_3/(2i)$, each once, and $$H_j\otimes H_\ell \cong H_{j+\ell}\oplus H_{j+\ell-1}\oplus\cdots\oplus H_{\|j-\ell\|}.$$ In particular, the tangent, plus two-form, and plus trace-free symmetric-matrix representations have types $$\left(\frac12,\frac12\right),\qquad (1,0),\qquad (2,0).$$ The Casimir operators and the orthogonal projections onto these irreducible types are parallel bundle endomorphisms.
  - Explanation: The representation rules determine parallel invariant projections without a spin-structure assumption.
- **C05** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-quadratic-curvature-action)): If $A$ has smallest eigenvalue $-x$, then $$Q_j(A)\le \frac{x(j+1)}{2j-1}\operatorname{Id}.$$ Moreover, $Q_2(A)=B_F(A)$. The compression of $B_F(A)$, acting on an $F$ factor, to a highest-spin coupling of total plus spin $j$ is $Q_j(A)$, provided the operator acts trivially on any additional irreducible minus factor.
  - Explanation: The least eigenvalue bounds the curvature action on the indicated representations.
- **C06** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-weyl-derivative-equations)): The tensor $Z=\nabla T$ belongs to the parallel subbundle $G$ of type $(5/2,1/2)$. On this bundle, put $B_{\mathrm{op}}=Q_{5/2}(T)$. Then $$\Delta T=6(T-N),\qquad N=(T^2)_0=T^2-2v^2\operatorname{Id},$$ and $$\Delta Z=(13-20B_{\mathrm{op}})Z. \tag{1}\label{eq:1}$$ Every statement has an exchanged counterpart for $U$.
  - Explanation: The Bianchi constraint restricts the derivative bundle and yields the displayed differential equations.
- **C07** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-gradient-parametrization)): There are unique vectors $k,l$ such that $$\begin{gathered} Z_{ii}=s_i p-\sqrt3\,\widetilde s_i k,\qquad Z_{jk}=-I_i\left(l+s_i k+\frac{\widetilde s_i}{\sqrt3}p\right), \qquad (i,j,k)\ \text{cyclic},\\ e=\frac53\|p\|^2+5\|k\|^2+\|l\|^2. \end{gathered} \tag{2}\label{eq:2}$$ More generally, for any fixed normalized trace-free diagonal triple $s$, the same formulas parametrize the entire algebraic space $G$ by three arbitrary real four-vectors.
  - Explanation: The derivative constraint has explicit vector coordinates with an exact energy decomposition.
- **C08** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-weak-norm-equation)): The function $e/v$, defined to be zero on $\{v=0\}$, belongs to $L^1(M)$, and $$\Delta v=6v(1-\sigma v)+\frac{e-\|p\|^2}{v} \tag{3}\label{eq:3}$$ holds as an equality of distributions with an $L^1$ density. The quotient is zero almost everywhere on the zero set. This identity may be tested against bounded Lipschitz functions. The corresponding assertions hold for $b$.
  - Explanation: Regularization gives an integrable weak norm equation across the zero set.
- **C09** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-weighted-variance)): For every real $a\in H^1(M)$, $$\operatorname{Var}(a) :=\mathbf E\bigl[(a-\mathbf E a)^2\bigr] \le\mathbf E\frac{\|\nabla a\|^2}{K}, \qquad K=4+v^2+b^2. \tag{4}\label{eq:4}$$
  - Explanation: The Weyl norms strengthen the scalar variance estimate through a positive weight.
- **C10** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-sharp-sobolev)): For every real $f\in H^1(M)$, $$\bigl(\mathbf E\|f\|^4\bigr)^{1/2} \le\mathbf E\left(f^2+\frac12\|\nabla f\|^2\right).$$
  - Explanation: Normalized volume gives the displayed Sobolev inequality.
- **C11** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-weyl-gap)): For either Weyl block, $$v\equiv0\qquad\text{or}\qquad \mathbf E v^2\ge1. \tag{5}\label{eq:5}$$ If $T\not\equiv0$ and $\mathbf E v^2=1$, then $\nabla T=0$ and $v=\sigma=1$. The corresponding statements hold for $U$.
  - Explanation: A nonzero Weyl block has the stated moment gap, whose equality forces parallelness.
- **C12** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-characteristic-identities)): Let $n_+$ and $n_-$ be the finite nonnegative integer dimensions of the spaces of harmonic self-dual and anti-self-dual two-forms. The Euler characteristic and signature of $M$ are, respectively, $$\chi=2+n_++n_-,\qquad \tau=n_+-n_-.$$ Then $$\begin{aligned} 8\pi^2\chi &=6\operatorname{Vol}(M) \bigl(1+\mathbf E(v^2+b^2)\bigr),\\ 12\pi^2\tau &=6\operatorname{Vol}(M)\,\mathbf E(v^2-b^2). \end{aligned} \tag{6}\label{eq:6}$$
  - Explanation: Characteristic numbers relate volume to the Weyl second moments.
- **C13** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-upper-volume-deficit)): One has $$\frac{V_M}{8\pi^2/3}\le 1-\frac{3}{40}s_0. \tag{7}\label{eq:7}$$
  - Explanation: The total Weyl second moment forces an upper volume deficit.
- **C14** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-geodesic-averages)): At every time along the geodesic flow, $$\mathcal E\|A\|^2=\frac32s_0,\qquad \mathcal E\|A'\|^2\le\frac32\,\mathcal E\|A\|^2. \tag{8}\label{eq:8}$$
  - Explanation: Geodesic-flow averages relate normal curvature to the Weyl moments and derivative energies.
- **C15** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-upper-polar)): One has $$V_M\le2\pi^2\int_0^\pi\mathcal E D_c(t)\,dt.$$
  - Explanation: Polar integration before conjugacy bounds the total volume.
- **C16** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-discrete-jacobi)): Fix a smooth symmetric normal curvature matrix on $[0,t]$, put $a=t/N$, and let $\mathcal L_N$ be the block tridiagonal matrix on nodes $1,\ldots,N-1$ with diagonal blocks $2\mathrm{Id}-a^2\mathcal R(ia)$ and off-diagonal blocks $-\mathrm{Id}$. Then $$a^3\det\mathcal L_N\longrightarrow D(t).$$ If $t$ is before the first conjugate time, $\mathcal L_N$ is positive definite for all sufficiently large $N$. The Jacobi convergence is uniform over families with uniformly bounded coefficients and first derivatives.
  - Explanation: Finite Dirichlet determinants converge to the normal Jacobi determinant.
- **C17** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-upper-radial)): For $0<t<\pi$, set $$\beta=\frac{t^2}{\pi^2-t^2},\qquad B=\frac2t\int_0^t A(s)\sin^2(\pi s/t)\,ds.$$ Before conjugacy, $$D(t)\le\sin^3t\,\det(\mathrm{Id}-\beta B), \qquad \mathrm{Id}-\beta B\succeq0.$$ At every time $0<t<\pi$, $$1-\frac{D_c(t)}{\sin^3t} \ge\frac16\min\{1,\beta^2\max(1,3-\beta)\}\,\|B\|^2.$$
  - Explanation: A sine-mode compression controls the radial determinant and its loss.
- **C18** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-sine-minorant)): Put $$a_0=\frac{157}{50},\qquad b_0=\frac{22}{7},\qquad S(z)=a_0z-\frac{(b_0z)^3}{6} +\frac{(a_0z)^5}{120}-\frac{(b_0z)^7}{5040}.$$ The constants satisfy $a_0<\pi<b_0$, and $0\le S(z)\le\sin(\pi z)$ for $0\le z\le1/2$. Its coefficients are $$S(z)=\frac{157}{50}z-\frac{5324}{1029}z^3 +\frac{95388992557}{37500000000}z^5 -\frac{155897368}{259416045}z^7.$$
  - Explanation: A rational polynomial minorizes sine on the stated interval.
- **C19** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-moment-obstruction)): Every probability measure $\mu$ on $[0,2]$ satisfying $$\int z^2\,d\mu\ge1,\qquad \int(28z+6z^2)\,d\mu\le25$$ satisfies $$16\left(\int H\,d\mu\right)^2-\int\Phi\,d\mu<0.$$
  - Explanation: The constrained moment problem has a strictly negative functional.
- **C20** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-balanced-moments)): If neither Weyl block is identically zero, then $\tau=0$ and $\mathbf E v^2=\mathbf E b^2$.
  - Explanation: Nonvanishing of both blocks forces balanced moments and vanishing signature.
- **C21** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-hessian-components)): Let $\mathcal H=T^*M\otimes G$, and write $X=\nabla Z$, so $X_{ij}=\nabla_i\nabla_jT$. Then $$X=Y+D_0, \qquad Y\text{ has type }(3,1),\qquad D_0\text{ has type }(2,0).$$ If $\operatorname{Tr}:\mathcal H\to F$ is contraction of the two derivative slots and $\operatorname{Tr}^*$ its adjoint, then $$\operatorname{Tr}\operatorname{Tr}^*=\frac{12}{5}\mathrm{Id}, \qquad D_0=\frac5{12}\operatorname{Tr}^*\Delta T, \qquad \|D_0\|^2=\frac5{12}\|\Delta T\|^2.$$ All the type projections in this statement are parallel.
  - Explanation: The actual Weyl Hessian has a free component and a prescribed trace component.
- **C22** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-hessian-operator)): The combined compressed operator $\mathcal P$ preserves all four types in $\mathcal H$. On $(3,1)$ it satisfies $\mathcal P\ge(4-2x-2y)\operatorname{Id}\ge0$, and on the trace type it satisfies $$\mathcal P\operatorname{Tr}^* =\operatorname{Tr}^*(4-B_F(T)).$$ The individual derivative-slot and matrix-slot summands need not preserve the four types.
  - Explanation: The combined curvature operator preserves the types and remains nonnegative at the sectional boundary.
- **C23** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-weighted-hessian)): For every global Lipschitz scalar weight $m\ge m_0>0$, $$0\le\mathbf E\left[ mG_v(p,k,l) -\frac56\sum_i m_i\, \frac{\operatorname{tr}\bigl(Z_i\sum_jZ_j^2\bigr)}6 +\frac{\langle\mathcal P(L_m)_Y,(L_m)_Y\rangle}{24m} \right], \tag{10}\label{eq:10}$$ where $i,j$ in the middle term are derivative indices and $$G_v=\left(-42-3b^2-\frac{10}{3}v^2\right)e +75B+\frac{25}{3}C_N-\frac{100}{3}E_B +5v^2\|p\|^2.$$ More precisely, if $\mathcal W_v(m)$ denotes the integrand in <a href="#eq:10" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:10">[eq:10]</a>, then the exact identity is $$\mathbf E\mathcal W_v(m) =\mathbf E\left[\frac m6\left\langle \mathcal P\left(Y+\frac{(L_m)_Y}{2m}\right), Y+\frac{(L_m)_Y}{2m}\right\rangle\right]. \tag{10a}\label{eq:weighted-hessian-square}$$
  - Explanation: A weighted Hessian square yields an integrated inequality in first derivatives.
- **C24** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-hessian-matrices)): At a point where $v>0$, use the three vector parameters $p,k,l$ in <a href="#eq:2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:2">[eq:2]</a>. Then $G_v$ is the quadratic form, with scalar entries multiplying vector dot products, whose matrix is $$\begin{pmatrix} -70-5b^2+100\sigma v-25v^2 &-100\sqrt3\rho v&-35\rho v^2/\sqrt3\\ -100\sqrt3\rho v &-210-15b^2-300\sigma v-190v^2&-75v-35\sigma v^2\\ -35\rho v^2/\sqrt3 &-75v-35\sigma v^2&-42-3b^2-10v^2 \end{pmatrix}. \tag{11}\label{eq:11}$$
  - Explanation: The invariant first-derivative form has the displayed coordinate matrix.
- **C25** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-cubic-contraction)): For every real tangent vector $a$, with $Z_a=\sum_i a_iZ_i$, $$\left\|\frac{\operatorname{tr}\bigl(Z_a\sum_jZ_j^2\bigr)}6\right\| \le \frac45\sqrt{\frac35}\,\|a\|e^{3/2}. \tag{13}\label{eq:13}$$
  - Explanation: The Bianchi constraint controls the cubic derivative contraction.
- **C26** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-real-vector-projections)): Let $P_+$ be the projection in $\mathcal H$ onto total plus spin $3$, retaining both possible minus spins. For any real tangent vector $a$ and any $Z\in G$, $$\|P_+(a\otimes Z)\|^2=\frac7{12}\|a\|^2\|Z\|^2,$$ $$\left\langle\widetilde QP_+(a\otimes Z),P_+(a\otimes Z)\right\rangle =\frac12\|a\|^2\langle Q_{5/2}(T)Z,Z\rangle.$$
  - Explanation: Real-vector tensor products have the stated projection norm and curvature expectation.
- **C27** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-hessian-projection-cost)): For the weight <a href="#eq:12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:12">[eq:12]</a>, $$\frac{\langle\mathcal P(L_m)_Y,(L_m)_Y\rangle}{24m} \le\frac9{64}\|p-q\|^2 \left[\frac7{12}(4+y)e-\frac54B\right]. \tag{14}\label{eq:14}$$ The bracket on the right is nonnegative. Both assertions remain valid under the closed inequalities $v+b\le2$ and $x+y\le2$.
  - Explanation: The weight derivative has a controlled nonnegative projection cost.
- **C28** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-coupling-estimates)): For this decomposition, define $$r=R_v\|p\|^2+R_b\|q\|^2,\qquad j=(\|p\|^2+\|q\|^2) \left(\frac{\|p\|^2}{v}+\frac{\|q\|^2}{b}\right).$$ Then, almost everywhere, $$\mathcal I_4\le-cj,\qquad \mathcal I_2\le2\Theta r,\qquad \mathcal I_0\le-\frac{\Theta^2}{c}.$$ The corresponding derivative summands are assigned value zero on a zero set, as in <a href="#eq:3" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:3">[eq:3]</a>. The total integrand and the bounds are integrable, and $\mathbf E\mathcal I\ge0$.
  - Explanation: Quartic, quadratic and potential bounds combine into the rigidity estimate.
- **C29** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-constant-norms)): On a closed, simply connected, oriented four-manifold with $\operatorname{Ric}=3g$, nonnegative sectional curvature, and neither Weyl block identically zero, one has $v=b=1$ everywhere.
  - Explanation: The Weyl norms become constant under the stated global hypotheses.
- **C30** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-parallel-blocks)): Both $T$ and $U$ are parallel, with eigenvalues $(2,-1,-1)$.
  - Explanation: The equality case makes both Weyl blocks parallel with the displayed spectrum.
- **C31** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-bernstein-conversion)): Let $p$ be a polynomial of degree at most $N$ on an interval $[r,s]$, and write $$p(r+(s-r)z)=\sum_{j=0}^N a_jz^j.$$ Its degree-$N$ Bernstein expansion is $$p(r+(s-r)z)=\sum_{i=0}^N h_i\binom Ni z^i(1-z)^{N-i}, \qquad h_i=\sum_{j=0}^i a_j\frac{\binom ij}{\binom Nj}.$$ In particular, $h_i\ge\eta$ for all $i$ implies $p\ge\eta$ on $[r,s]$.* *For a polynomial on a triangle with ordered vertices $p_\ell=(r_\ell,s_\ell)$, set $$V=r_3+(r_1-r_3)z+(r_2-r_3)w,\qquad W=s_3+(s_1-s_3)z+(s_2-s_3)w.$$ If $P(z,w)=p(V,W)=\sum_{d,e}P_{de}z^dw^e$ has degree at most $N$, then its normalized degree-$N$ triangular coefficients are $$[p]_{ij}=s_{ij}(P):= \sum_{d=0}^i\sum_{e=0}^j \frac{\binom id\binom je} {\binom N{d+e}\binom{d+e}d}\,P_{de}, \qquad i,j\ge0,\quad i+j\le N.$$ On $z,w\ge0$, $z+w\le1$, the polynomial is the sum of these coefficients times the nonnegative basis functions $$\frac{N!}{i!j!(N-i-j)!} z^iw^j(1-z-w)^{N-i-j}.$$ These basis functions sum to one. The same expansion applies entrywise to a symmetric polynomial matrix: positive semidefiniteness of every coefficient matrix implies positive semidefiniteness throughout the triangle.
  - Explanation: Bernstein coefficients convert finite interval and triangle expansions into scalar and matrix positivity.
- **C32** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-scalar-certificates)): For the polynomials in Section <a href="#sec:coupling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:coupling">6</a>, the following bounds hold: $$\begin{gathered} C_0>0,\qquad 4C_0\left((h-A_0)(2-v)+\frac25hv\right) -(2-v)(2A_0+L_0)^2>0\quad(0\le v\le2),\\ G,H>0\quad(0\le u\le1),\qquad Z_*\le0\quad(0\le u\le1/16),\\ Z_*^2\le64A^2u^2GH\quad(1/16\le u\le1). \end{gathered}$$
  - Explanation: Exact scalar coefficient bounds support the quartic comparison.
- **C33** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-matrix-certificates)): The symmetric matrix represented by $\mathcal A$ is positive semidefinite on $$\mathcal D=\{(v,b):v,b\ge0,\ v+b\le2\}.$$ The symmetric matrix represented by $\mathcal B$ is positive semidefinite on $$\mathcal D_+=\{(v,b)\in\mathcal D:v\ge b\}.$$ On $\mathcal D_+$ one also has $$L_v\ge0,\qquad F+12c\delta^2L_b\ge0.$$
  - Explanation: Triangular matrix and boundary certificates support the quadratic and potential comparisons.
- **C34** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-potential-certificate)): The polynomial $F=cE_0-\Theta^2$ is nonnegative on $\mathcal D$.
  - Explanation: The potential polynomial is nonnegative throughout the Weyl-norm domain.

          "exact_quote": "A spin-$j$ representation has weights $-j,-j+1,\\ldots,j$ for $\\Sigma_3/(2i)$, each once, and $$H_j\\otimes H_\\ell\n \\cong H_{j+\\ell}\\oplus H_{j+\\ell-1}\\oplus\\cdots\\oplus H_{|j-\\ell|}.$$ In particular, the tangent, plus two-form, and plus trace-free symmetric-matrix representations have types $$\\left(\\frac12,\\frac12\\right),\\qquad (1,0),\\qquad (2,0).$$ The Casimir operators and the orthogonal projections onto these irreducible types are parallel bundle endomorphisms.",
          "source_id": "PM-348.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "C04",
      "kind": "conditional_result",
      "quantifiers": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-spin-algebra",
          "exact_quote": "A spin-$j$ representation has weights $-j,-j+1,\\ldots,j$ for $\\Sigma_3/(2i)$, each once, and $$H_j\\otimes H_\\ell\n \\cong H_{j+\\ell}\\oplus H_{j+\\ell-1}\\oplus\\cdots\\oplus H_{|j-\\ell|}.$$ In particular, the tangent, plus two-form, and plus trace-free symmetric-matrix representations have types $$\\left(\\frac12,\\frac12\\right),\\qquad (1,0),\\qquad (2,0).$$ The Casimir operators and the orthogonal projections onto these irreducible types are parallel bundle endomorphisms.",
          "source_id": "PM-348.1"
        }
      ],
      "statement_anchor": "stmt-lem-spin-algebra"
    },

        "exact_quote": "If $A$ has smallest eigenvalue $-x$, then $$Q_j(A)\\le \\frac{x(j+1)}{2j-1}\\operatorname{Id}.$$ Moreover, $Q_2(A)=B_F(A)$.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-weyl-derivative-equations",
        "exact_quote": "The tensor $Z=\\nabla T$ belongs to the parallel subbundle $G$ of type $(5/2,1/2)$. On this bundle, put $B_{\\mathrm{op}}=Q_{5/2}(T)$.       },

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-gradient-parametrization",
        "exact_quote": "There are unique vectors $k,l$ such that $$\\begin{gathered}\n Z_{ii}=s_i p-\\sqrt3\\,\\widetilde s_i k,\\qquad\n Z_{jk}=-I_i\\left(l+s_i k+\\frac{\\widetilde s_i}{\\sqrt3}p\\right),\n \\qquad (i,j,k)\\ \\text{cyclic},\\\\\n e=\\frac53|p|^2+5|k|^2+|l|^2.\n \\end{gathered}\n \\tag{2}\\label{eq:2}$$ More generally, for any fixed normalized trace-free diagonal triple $s$, the same formulas parametrize the entire algebraic space $G$ by three arbitrary real four-vectors.",
        "source_id": "PM-348.1"
      },

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-characteristic-identities",
        "exact_quote": "Let $n_+$ and $n_-$ be the finite nonnegative integer dimensions of the spaces of harmonic self-dual and anti-self-dual two-forms. The Euler characteristic and signature of $M$ are, respectively, $$\\chi=2+n_++n_-,\\qquad \\tau=n_+-n_-.$$ Then $$\\begin{aligned}\n 8\\pi^2\\chi\n   &=6\\operatorname{Vol}(M)\n        \\bigl(1+\\mathbf E(v^2+b^2)\\bigr),\\\\\n 12\\pi^2\\tau\n   &=6\\operatorname{Vol}(M)\\,\\mathbf E(v^2-b^2).\n \\end{aligned}\n \\tag{6}\\label{eq:6}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-348.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-prop-characteristic-identities",
          "exact_quote": "Let $n_+$ and $n_-$ be the finite nonnegative integer dimensions of the spaces of harmonic self-dual and anti-self-dual two-forms.           "source_id": "PM-348.1"
        }

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-discrete-jacobi",
        "exact_quote": "Fix a smooth symmetric normal curvature matrix on $[0,t]$, put $a=t/N$, and let $\\mathcal L_N$ be the block tridiagonal matrix on nodes $1,\\ldots,N-1$ with diagonal blocks $2\\mathrm{Id}-a^2\\mathcal R(ia)$ and off-diagonal blocks $-\\mathrm{Id}$. Then $$a^3\\det\\mathcal L_N\\longrightarrow D(t).$$ If $t$ is before the first conjugate time, $\\mathcal L_N$ is positive definite for all sufficiently large $N$.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-upper-radial",
        "exact_quote": "For $0<t<\\pi$, set $$\\beta=\\frac{t^2}{\\pi^2-t^2},\\qquad\n B=\\frac2t\\int_0^t A(s)\\sin^2(\\pi s/t)\\,ds.$$ Before conjugacy, $$D(t)\\le\\sin^3t\\,\\det(\\mathrm{Id}-\\beta B),\n \\qquad \\mathrm{Id}-\\beta B\\succeq0.$$ At every time $0<t<\\pi$, $$1-\\frac{D_c(t)}{\\sin^3t}\n \\ge\\frac16\\min\\{1,\\beta^2\\max(1,3-\\beta)\\}\\,|B|^2.$$",

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-hessian-components",
        "exact_quote": "Let $\\mathcal H=T^*M\\otimes G$, and write $X=\\nabla Z$, so $X_{ij}=\\nabla_i\\nabla_jT$.         "source_id": "PM-348.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

          "source_id": "PM-348.1"
        }

        "source_id": "PM-348.1"

      "exact_statement": "For every global Lipschitz scalar weight $m\\ge m_0>0$, $$0\\le\\mathbf E\\left[\n mG_v(p,k,l)\n -\\frac56\\sum_i m_i\\,\n    \\frac{\\operatorname{tr}\\bigl(Z_i\\sum_jZ_j^2\\bigr)}6\n +\\frac{\\langle\\mathcal P(L_m)_Y,(L_m)_Y\\rangle}{24m}\n \\right],\n \\tag{10}\\label{eq:10}$$ where $i,j$ in the middle term are derivative indices and $$G_v=\\left(-42-3b^2-\\frac{10}{3}v^2\\right)e\n       +75B+\\frac{25}{3}C_N-\\frac{100}{3}E_B\n       +5v^2|p|^2.$$ More precisely, if $\\mathcal W_v(m)$ denotes the integrand in <a href=\"#eq:10\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:10\">[eq:10]</a>, then the exact identity is $$\\mathbf E\\mathcal W_v(m)\n =\\mathbf E\\left[\\frac m6\\left\\langle\n \\mathcal P\\left(Y+\\frac{(L_m)_Y}{2m}\\right),\n               Y+\\frac{(L_m)_Y}{2m}\\right\\rangle\\right].\n \\tag{10a}\\label{eq:weighted-hessian-square}$$",

        "exact_quote": "For this decomposition, define $$r=R_v|p|^2+R_b|q|^2,\\qquad\n j=(|p|^2+|q|^2)\n       \\left(\\frac{|p|^2}{v}+\\frac{|q|^2}{b}\\right).$$ Then, almost everywhere, $$\\mathcal I_4\\le-cj,\\qquad\n \\mathcal I_2\\le2\\Theta r,\\qquad\n \\mathcal I_0\\le-\\frac{\\Theta^2}{c}.$$ The corresponding derivative summands are assigned value zero on a zero set, as in <a href=\"#eq:3\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:3\">[eq:3]</a>. <a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Normalized volume and expectation** ([source](#sec:preliminaries)): Write $$d\nu=\frac{d\operatorname{vol}_g}{\operatorname{Vol}(M)}, \qquad \mathbf E f=\int_M f\,d\nu.$$
- **Hodge-star eigenspaces** ([source](#pm-h-6)): The Hodge-star eigenspaces $\Lambda^+$ and $\Lambda^-$ have eigenvalues $+1$ and $-1$, respectively.
- **Weyl norm and spectral variables** ([source](#stmt-lem-curvature-blocks)): Put $$x=-\min_s t_s,\qquad y=-\min_s u_s,\qquad v=\frac{\|T\|}{\sqrt6},\qquad b=\frac{\|U\|}{\sqrt6},$$ where the matrix norms are Hilbert–Schmidt norms.
- **Spin representation** ([source](#pm-h-7)): A complexified irreducible representation has *spin $j$* when $$\sum_{s=1}^3\Sigma_s^2=-4j(j+1)\operatorname{Id}.$$
- **Matrix-slot curvature operator** ([source](#pm-h-7)): For a symmetric trace-free matrix $A$, define the self-adjoint operator on $F$ $$B_F(A)S=\frac{AS+SA}{2} -\frac{\operatorname{tr}(AS)}3\operatorname{Id}.$$
- **Spin curvature operator** ([source](#pm-h-7)): On any plus spin-$j$ representation with $j\ge1$, define $$Q_j(A)=\frac{\sum_{r,s}A_{rs}\Sigma_r\Sigma_s}{4j(2j-1)}.$$
- **Derivative energies** ([source](#pm-h-8)): Write $$p=\nabla v,\qquad q=\nabla b,\qquad e=\frac{\|\nabla T\|^2}{6},\qquad \bar e=\frac{\|\nabla U\|^2}{6}.$$
- **Volume and total Weyl moment** ([source](#sec:volume)): Write $V_M=\operatorname{Vol}(M,g)$ and $s_0=\mathbf E(v^2+b^2)$.
- **Preconjugate determinant** ([source](#pm-h-14)): Put $D_c(t)=D(t)$ before the first conjugate time and $D_c(t)=0$ at and after it.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-prop-balanced-moments) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-balanced-moments): Combine characteristic identities, volume deficit and constrained moments. -> Balanced Weyl second moments.
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-prop-coupling-estimates) / [proof](#sec:coupling): Combine weighted Hessian squares with norm tests and polynomial certificates. -> Quartic, quadratic and potential bounds.
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-prop-constant-norms) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-constant-norms): Complete the square and use strictness away from norm level sets. -> Constant Weyl norms.
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-prop-parallel-blocks) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-parallel-blocks): Use the weak norm equation and nonnegativity of both terms. -> Parallel Weyl blocks with fixed spectrum.
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-parallel-blocks-2): Construct parallel eigendistributions and apply de Rham decomposition. -> Product of round spheres.

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **bernstein-interval**: Exact interval Bernstein coefficients reconstruct the polynomial; their common lower bound bounds it throughout the interval.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **sampling**: Infer polynomial positivity from sampled function values. -> A sample can miss a negative value between evaluation points.
- **geometry-overclaim**: Treat interval certificate code as verification of zero-plane rigidity. -> Polynomial conversion does not establish the geometric and analytic hypotheses or the full proof.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The interval Bernstein expansion is a finite constructive result. Its coefficient lower bound supplies a polynomial sign certificate; this implementation does not verify the geometric rigidity theorem.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON array of rational polynomial interval requests.
# PRECONDITIONS: 0 <= degree <= N <= 8; rational r < s; at most 1000 requests.
# OUTPUTS: Exact Bernstein coefficients and their minimum, or a contract error.
# TIME: O(N^2) rational operations per request.
# SPACE: O(N) rationals per request, plus the output batch.
# ARITHMETIC: Arbitrary precision fmpq rational arithmetic.
# FAILURE: Invalid contracts return an error; no approximate certificate is emitted.
import json, sys
from math import comb
from flint import fmpq

def convert(item):
    try:
        N = item['N']
        c = [fmpq(q) for q in item['c']]
        r, s = fmpq(item['r']), fmpq(item['s'])
        if type(N) is not int or not 0 <= N <= 8 or not c or len(c) > N+1 or not r < s:
            return {'error': 'contract'}
        a = [sum((c[k]*comb(k,j)*r**(k-j)*(s-r)**j for k in range(j,len(c))), fmpq(0)) for j in range(N+1)]
        h = [sum((a[j]*fmpq(comb(i,j),comb(N,j)) for j in range(i+1)), fmpq(0)) for i in range(N+1)]
        return {'h': [str(q) for q in h], 'lower': str(min(h))}
    except (ValueError, TypeError, KeyError, ZeroDivisionError):
        return {'error': 'contract'}

batch = json.load(sys.stdin)
if not isinstance(batch,list) or len(batch)>1000:
    raise ValueError('batch contract')
print(json.dumps([convert(item) for item in batch],sort_keys=True))
```

    "input_contract": "JSON batch of at most 1000 requests; each has N in 0..8, a nonempty list c of rational strings with len(c)<=N+1, and rational strings r<s. Contract violations return an error.",

    "oracle_code": "import json as js, sys as osys\nfrom fractions import Fraction as Rat\nfrom math import factorial\n\ndef multiply(left,right):\n    result=[Rat(0)]*(len(left)+len(right)-1)\n    for u,x in enumerate(left):\n        for v,y in enumerate(right):\n            result[u+v]+=x*y\n    return result\n\ndef power(poly,exponent):\n    answer=[Rat(1)]\n    for unused in range(exponent):\n        answer=multiply(answer,poly)\n    return answer\n\ntry:\n    requests=js.load(open(osys.argv[1]))\n    actual=js.load(open(osys.argv[2]))\n    if actual != js.loads(osys.argv[3]) or len(actual)!=len(requests):\n        raise ValueError('expected output mismatch')\n    for req,ans in zip(requests,actual):\n        n=req['N']; coeff=list(map(Rat,req['c'])); lo=Rat(req['r']); hi=Rat(req['s'])\n        valid=type(n) is int and 0<=n<=8 and 0<len(coeff)<=n+1 and lo<hi\n        if not valid:\n            if ans != {'error':'contract'}: raise ValueError('invalid request accepted')\n            continue\n        hs=list(map(Rat,ans['h']))\n        if len(hs)!=n+1 or Rat(ans['lower'])!=min(hs): raise ValueError('lower bound')\n        reconstructed=[Rat(0)]*(n+1)\n        for index,weight in enumerate(hs):\n            basis=multiply(power([Rat(0),Rat(1)],index),power([Rat(1),Rat(-1)],n-index))\n            scale=Rat(factorial(n),factorial(index)*factorial(n-index))\n            for exponent,number in enumerate(basis): reconstructed[exponent]+=weight*scale*number\n        transformed=[Rat(0)]*(n+1)\n        for exponent,number in enumerate(coeff):\n            term=power([lo,hi-lo],exponent)\n            for slot,value in enumerate(term): transformed[slot]+=number*value\n        if reconstructed != transformed: raise ValueError('polynomial identity')\nexcept Exception:\n    osys.exit(7)\nosys.exit(0)\n",
    "output_contract": "Exact rational Bernstein coefficient strings h and their minimum lower; invalid requests return a contract error.",
    "reason": "The interval Bernstein expansion is a finite constructive result. Its coefficient lower bound supplies a polynomial sign certificate; this implementation does not verify the geometric rigidity theorem.",
    "reference_code": "# INPUTS: JSON array of rational polynomial interval requests.\n# PRECONDITIONS: 0 <= degree <= N <= 8; rational r < s; at most 1000 requests.\n# OUTPUTS: Exact Bernstein coefficients and their minimum, or a contract error.\n# TIME: O(N^2) rational operations per request.\n# SPACE: O(N) rationals per request, plus the output batch.\n# ARITHMETIC: Arbitrary precision fmpq rational arithmetic.\n# FAILURE: Invalid contracts return an error; no approximate certificate is emitted.\nimport json, sys\nfrom math import comb\nfrom flint import fmpq\n\ndef convert(item):\n    try:\n        N = item['N']\n        c = [fmpq(q) for q in item['c']]\n        r, s = fmpq(item['r']), fmpq(item['s'])\n        if type(N) is not int or not 0 <= N <= 8 or not c or len(c) > N+1 or not r < s:\n            return {'error': 'contract'}\n        a = [sum((c[k]*comb(k,j)*r**(k-j)*(s-r)**j for k in range(j,len(c))), fmpq(0)) for j in range(N+1)]\n        h = [sum((a[j]*fmpq(comb(i,j),comb(N,j)) for j in range(i+1)), fmpq(0)) for i in range(N+1)]\n        return {'h': [str(q) for q in h], 'lower': str(min(h))}\n    except (ValueError, TypeError, KeyError, ZeroDivisionError):\n        return {'error': 'contract'}\n\nbatch = json.load(sys.stdin)\nif not isinstance(batch,list) or len(batch)>1000:\n    raise ValueError('batch contract')\nprint(json.dumps([convert(item) for item in batch],sort_keys=True))\n",

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | True | moderate | implementable | inferred | The rational Bernstein expansions support software that checks the displayed scalar and matrix positivity certificates. This transfer covers finite algebraic checks, not automatic verification of the geometric hypotheses. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | A game or simulation state is not identified with a closed Einstein manifold satisfying the curvature assumptions. An application contract is ABSENT. |
| tools | True | moderate | implementable | inferred | An exact certificate inspection tool can reproduce the rational expansions and distinguish a sufficient positivity certificate from an inconclusive coefficient test. |
| algorithms | True | strong | implementable | inferred | The interval and triangular conversion formulas give finite algorithms for sufficient scalar lower bounds and matrix positive semidefiniteness on specified domains. |
| data_structures | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | Coefficient arrays with convolution represent the finite polynomial expansions used by the certificates. This is a representation for these calculations rather than a new general data structure. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The proof specifies exact algebraic certificates but no accelerator architecture, arithmetic format, or hardware performance contract. Such a contract is ABSENT. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The stated rigidity concerns Riemannian curvature and parallel subbundles; a security primitive or adversarial model is ABSENT. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Neither the geometric rigidity conclusion nor the exact coefficient criterion establishes a learning or generalisation guarantee. A learning contract is ABSENT. |
| optimisation_operations_research | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | The matrix Bernstein criterion can certify a specified symmetric polynomial matrix as positive semidefinite throughout a triangle. It supplies a sufficient constraint certificate, not an optimisation algorithm or an optimality guarantee. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The global Riemannian splitting does not describe distributed processes, communication, or fault assumptions. A distributed systems contract is ABSENT. |
| other (Riemannian differential geometry) | True | strong | adaptable | stated | The rigidity theorem directly identifies the universal cover of a connected smooth closed Einstein four-manifold with nonnegative sectional curvature and a zero-curvature plane. The result is a geometric classification contract. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L01** (hypothesis): The rigidity conclusion requires a connected smooth closed Riemannian manifold with the stated Einstein normalization and nonnegative sectional curvature. Blocked use: ['Applying the theorem to noncompact manifolds, manifolds with boundary, nonsmooth spaces, or metrics without the Einstein hypothesis.', 'Transferring the conclusion to Lorentzian metrics without a separate theorem.']
- **L02** (hypothesis): The product conclusion in the zero-plane theorem requires a zero-curvature tangent plane; nonnegative curvature alone is the hypothesis of the broader classification. Blocked use: ['Selecting the product geometry solely from nonnegative sectional curvature.']
- **L03** (counterexample_scope): The asserted isometry concerns the universal Riemannian cover. It does not identify the original manifold with that product. Blocked use: ['Discarding the distinction between a manifold and its universal Riemannian cover.']
- **L04** (edge_case): The constant-norm argument assumes that neither Weyl block vanishes identically; individual blocks may still have zeros. Blocked use: ['Using the constant-norm conclusion in the half-conformally-flat branch.']
- **L05** (edge_case): At a Weyl-norm zero, the weak equation uses a prescribed almost-everywhere zero quotient and an integrable distributional density. Blocked use: ['Dividing by a vanishing Weyl norm pointwise.', 'Replacing the distributional identity with an unproved smooth norm identity.']
- **L06** (edge_case): The compressed Hessian form is nonnegative at the sectional boundary; strict positive definiteness is not supplied. Blocked use: ['Assuming that equality forces the highest Hessian component to vanish merely because its quadratic form is nonnegative.']
- **L07** (approximation): The discrete Jacobi determinant statement is a convergence result. Its positive definiteness assertion requires preconjugacy and sufficiently large discretization size. Blocked use: ['Treating a finite discretization as the exact continuous determinant.', 'Assuming positivity for an arbitrary discretization or beyond the first conjugate time.']
- **L08** (edge_case): The upper radial comparison states its preconjugate determinant estimate on the specified open time interval. Blocked use: ['Using the untruncated determinant after conjugacy.', 'Substituting an endpoint into a formula with a singular denominator without a separate limiting argument.']
- **L09** (counterexample_scope): Bernstein coefficient positivity is a sufficient sign certificate; a failed coefficient test does not establish a negative polynomial value. Blocked use: ['Reporting failure of coefficient positivity as a mathematical counterexample.', 'Using sampled or rounded values as an exact rational certificate.']
- **L10** (hypothesis): Matrix coefficient certificates apply to their stated Weyl-norm domains. The certificate for the second comparison matrix uses the ordered half-domain. Blocked use: ['Extending a triangular certificate outside its domain.', 'Dropping the ordering condition without exchanging the block labels consistently.']
- **L11** (edge_case): The coefficient certificates retain boundary zeros rather than asserting strict positivity everywhere. Blocked use: ['Rejecting a valid positive semidefinite coefficient matrix solely because it is zero.', 'Claiming a strictly positive potential throughout the closed domain.']
- **L12** (nonconstructive): Finite polynomial checks establish the displayed algebraic signs. They do not by themselves establish the smooth global geometric hypotheses or the entire rigidity proof. Blocked use: ['Presenting a Bernstein conversion program as a decision procedure for arbitrary manifold rigidity.']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that a closed Einstein four-manifold with positive Einstein constant, nonnegative sectional curvature, and a zero-curvature plane has universal Riemannian cover isometric to a product of two round two-spheres. Under the normalization $`\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}\nolimits =3g`$, both spheres have radius $`1/\sqrt3`$. The proof extends coupled estimates for the two Weyl curvature blocks to the boundary of the sectional-curvature cone and determines their equality case.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

An Einstein metric has Ricci tensor equal to a constant multiple of the metric. In dimension four this fixes the sum of the sectional curvatures through each tangent direction, while leaving two independent Weyl curvature blocks. A basic rigidity problem asks how much nonnegative sectional curvature restricts those blocks. The product of two round surfaces illustrates the boundary case: planes tangent to a factor have positive curvature, whereas planes spanned by one direction from each factor have zero curvature. We prove that, for a closed Einstein four-manifold with positive Einstein constant, a single zero-curvature plane already forces this product geometry on the universal cover.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1** (Zero-plane rigidity). *Let $(M^4,g)$ be a connected smooth closed Riemannian manifold satisfying $$\operatorname{Ric}_g=3g,\qquad K_g(\sigma)\ge0$$ for every tangent two-plane $\sigma$. If $K_g(\sigma_0)=0$ for at least one tangent two-plane, then the universal Riemannian cover is isometric to $$S^2(1/\sqrt3)\times S^2(1/\sqrt3)$$ with its product metric. In particular, the tangent bundle of the universal cover is the orthogonal sum of two parallel rank-two subbundles.*

</div>

Here $S^2(r)$ denotes the round sphere of radius $r$, and closed means compact without boundary. No orientation, symmetry, or topological condition is assumed on $M$. The Einstein normalization only fixes the scale: for $\operatorname{Ric}=\lambda g$ with $\lambda>0$, the two radii are $1/\sqrt\lambda$.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Context and the boundary problem

The classification of positively curved Einstein four-manifolds has a long history. Berger’s rigidity under strict quarter pinching <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Berger1961](#ref-Berger1961)] was followed by quantitative curvature conditions of Yang, Costa, and Cao–Tran <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Yang2000](#ref-Yang2000), [Costa2004](#ref-Costa2004), [CaoTran2018](#ref-CaoTran2018)]. Gursky–LeBrun obtained the Fubini–Study conclusion under nonnegative sectional curvature and a nonzero positive-definite intersection form <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[GurskyLeBrun1999](#ref-GurskyLeBrun1999), Theorem A]. Their separate Weyl-norm gaps and analysis of the two Weyl blocks are important antecedents of the argument here. Scalar combinations of the two Weyl norms also appear in Yang’s coupling argument <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Yang2000](#ref-Yang2000), Section 6] and in Cao–Tran <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[CaoTran2018](#ref-CaoTran2018), Proposition 2.2]. Under nonnegative isotropic curvature, which is a different curvature condition, the Einstein local-symmetry theorem in dimension four is due to Micallef–Wang <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[MicallefWang1993](#ref-MicallefWang1993), Theorem 4.4]; Brendle established the analogous rigidity in higher dimensions <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Brendle2010](#ref-Brendle2010), Theorem 1].

Recent results treat additional topology or symmetry. Cheng proves the oriented positive-sectional classification when the Euler characteristic is at most three <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Cheng2026](#ref-Cheng2026), Theorem 1.2]; Gursky–Malchiodi obtain it under $2\chi-3|\tau|\le4$, where $\chi$ is the Euler characteristic and $\tau$ the signature <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[GurskyMalchiodi2026](#ref-GurskyMalchiodi2026), Theorem 1.1]. For nonnegative sectional curvature, Liu proves the classification of closed simply connected manifolds with a $T^2$-invariant Einstein metric <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Liu2025](#ref-Liu2025), Theorem 1.1]. Di Cerbo obtains the $S^2\times S^2$ universal cover when equality in a Weyl-norm bound holds at every point <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[DiCerbo2026](#ref-DiCerbo2026), Proposition 3.2]. The hypothesis of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> instead specifies one sectional-curvature plane at one point.

The companion manuscript *Positively curved Einstein four-manifolds <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[OpenAI2026P](#ref-OpenAI2026P)]*  proves the strictly positive classification without additional curvature or topological restrictions. Its coupled Weyl estimates provide the starting point of this paper. We reproduce the tensor calculations, upper volume comparison, moment argument, and polynomial inequalities needed here, with their attribution at the point of use. The extra issue is their behavior at the boundary of the sectional-curvature cone. An estimate proved for a strictly positive metric does not by itself settle that boundary case.

The argument also gives the full classification under nonnegative sectional curvature.

<a id="stmt-cor-nonnegative-classification"></a>
<div id="cor:nonnegative-classification" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2**. *Let $(M^4,g)$ be connected, smooth, and closed, with $\operatorname{Ric}=3g$ and nonnegative sectional curvature. Its universal Riemannian cover is one of the round $S^4$ of curvature $1$, the complex projective plane with its Fubini–Study metric normalized by $\operatorname{Ric}=3g$, or $S^2(1/\sqrt3)\times S^2(1/\sqrt3)$.*

</div>

Its proof is given at the end of Section <a href="#sec:classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:classification">7</a>.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Reduction and proof strategy

The universal Riemannian cover is complete and has $\operatorname{Ric}=3g$, so Bonnet–Myers makes it compact <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Myers1941](#ref-Myers1941)]. It is simply connected and therefore orientable. We work on this cover from now on and keep the notation $(M,g)$. The zero-curvature plane lifts to it.

Choose an orientation and let $\Lambda^+$ and $\Lambda^-$ be the rank-three eigenspaces of the Hodge star on two-forms. The Einstein curvature operator has blocks $\operatorname{Id}+T$ and $\operatorname{Id}+U$, where $T,U$ are symmetric and trace-free. They are the self-dual and anti-self-dual Weyl blocks. Set $$v=\frac{|T|}{\sqrt6},\qquad b=\frac{|U|}{\sqrt6},\qquad
 x=-\lambda_{\min}(T),\qquad y=-\lambda_{\min}(U).$$ All matrix norms are Hilbert–Schmidt norms. As proved in Lemma <a href="#lem:curvature-blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:curvature-blocks">3</a>, the smallest sectional curvature is $1-(x+y)/2$, and $$v\le x\le2v,\qquad b\le y\le2b,\qquad x+y\le2.$$ The stronger spectral inequality $x+y\le2$, not merely $v+b\le2$, is retained in the tensor estimates.

If one Weyl block vanished identically, the metric would be half-conformally flat. The classical compact positive-scalar Einstein classification of Hitchin <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Hitchin1981](#ref-Hitchin1981)], with conformal Einstein rigidity <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Obata1962](#ref-Obata1962)], would identify our simply connected cover with the standard $S^4$ or $\mathbb{CP}^2$ up to scale; see also <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Derdzinski1983](#ref-Derdzinski1983), Remark 7]. Both have strictly positive sectional curvature. We may therefore assume throughout the remaining proof that neither block vanishes identically. The zero-plane hypothesis is used only in this reduction: the remaining estimates assume only nonnegative sectional curvature and that neither block vanishes identically. Individual blocks may still vanish at some points.

Write $\mathbf E f$ for the integral of $f$ divided by the volume of $M$. The proof now has three stages. First, the separate gaps $\mathbf E v^2,\mathbf E b^2\ge1$, a weighted variance inequality, and an upper volume deficit force $$\mathbf E v^2=\mathbf E b^2.$$ Second, a weighted Hessian identity and explicit polynomial inequalities couple the two blocks and force $dv=db=0$ almost everywhere. Third, the gaps and $v+b\le2$ give $v=b=1$. The weak norm equations then imply that both Weyl blocks are parallel, each with spectrum $(2,-1,-1)$. Their simple eigenlines produce the parallel tangent splitting.

Three features allow these estimates to reach the boundary. The upper volume estimate integrates over the domain before the first conjugate point and needs no lower bound on the injectivity radius. The weighted Hessian identity needs a nonnegative quadratic form, so it remains valid when that form has a kernel. At a zero of one Weyl block, the pointwise algebraic estimates are obtained by introducing a small nonzero block and shrinking the opposite block while keeping its derivative data fixed. The decisive strictness then comes from the fact that the gradient of a Lipschitz function vanishes almost everywhere on a level set. This forces constant Weyl norms without requiring strict sectional positivity.

Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a> develops the curvature identities, including the weak norm equations at zeros. Sections <a href="#sec:analytic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:analytic">3</a> and <a href="#sec:volume" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:volume">4</a> establish the scalar estimates and moment balance. Sections <a href="#sec:hessian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:hessian">5</a> and <a href="#sec:coupling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:coupling">6</a> give the Hessian and coupling inequalities. Section <a href="#sec:classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:classification">7</a> resolves their equality case and proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. Appendix <a href="#sec:certificates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:certificates">8</a> supplies the exact polynomial certificates, including all boundary faces.

<a id="sec:preliminaries"></a>
### Curvature blocks and their covariant derivatives

Our first task is to express nonnegative sectional curvature and the differential Bianchi identity in a common set of variables. Throughout Sections <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a>–<a href="#sec:coupling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:coupling">6</a>, we work on the closed, simply connected, oriented cover obtained in Section <a href="#sec:introduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:introduction">1</a>, with $\operatorname{Ric}=3g$, $\sec_g\ge0$, and neither Weyl block identically zero. The curvature decomposition is classical; see Derdziński <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Derdzinski1983](#ref-Derdzinski1983), Section 2]. The derivative identities and their normalizations follow the treatment in <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[OpenAI2026P](#ref-OpenAI2026P), Lemmas 2.1–2.3 and Proposition 2.4]; we reproduce them because their exact constants enter the Hessian inequality. Write $$d\nu=\frac{d\operatorname{vol}_g}{\operatorname{Vol}(M)},
 \qquad \mathbf E f=\int_M f\,d\nu.$$ The Laplacian $\Delta$ is the trace of the second covariant derivative. Our curvature convention is $$[\nabla_i,\nabla_j]\xi_k=R_{ijka}\xi_a$$ for a covector, with $R_{ijij}>0$ on positively curved orthonormal two-planes. All tensor connections are induced by the Levi–Civita connection. We take covariant derivatives before choosing any pointwise eigenbasis.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### The two Weyl blocks

The Hodge-star eigenspaces $\Lambda^+$ and $\Lambda^-$ have eigenvalues $+1$ and $-1$, respectively. On an oriented Euclidean four-space, choose triples of skew orthogonal complex structures $I_1,I_2,I_3$ and $J_1,J_2,J_3$ satisfying $$I_1I_2=I_3=-I_2I_1,\qquad
 J_1J_2=J_3=-J_2J_1,$$ and their cyclic analogues, with every $I_s$ commuting with every $J_t$. The two triples can be constructed from left and right quaternionic multiplication. As two-forms, $I_s/\sqrt2$ and $J_s/\sqrt2$ are orthonormal bases of $\Lambda^+$ and $\Lambda^-$, respectively. Vector and covector indices are identified by the metric.

<a id="stmt-lem-curvature-blocks"></a>
<div id="lem:curvature-blocks" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Curvature blocks and nonnegative sectional curvature). *There are symmetric trace-free endomorphisms $T\in\operatorname{Sym}_0(\Lambda^+)$ and $U\in\operatorname{Sym}_0(\Lambda^-)$ such that the curvature operator is $$\mathcal R_{\Lambda^2}
 =\begin{pmatrix}\operatorname{Id}+T&0\\
                  0&\operatorname{Id}+U\end{pmatrix}.$$ At a point where these endomorphisms are diagonal, with eigenvalues $t_s,u_s$, the curvature tensor is $$R_{ikjl}
 =\frac12\sum_s(1+t_s)I_{s,ik}I_{s,jl}
  +\frac12\sum_s(1+u_s)J_{s,ik}J_{s,jl}.$$ Put $$x=-\min_s t_s,\qquad y=-\min_s u_s,\qquad
 v=\frac{|T|}{\sqrt6},\qquad b=\frac{|U|}{\sqrt6},$$ where the matrix norms are Hilbert–Schmidt norms. Then $$\sec_g\ge0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad x+y\le2,
 \qquad
 v\le x\le2v,\qquad b\le y\le2b.$$ Both Weyl blocks, viewed as curvature four-tensors, have zero divergence. In particular, for $Z=\nabla T$, whose matrix entries are one-forms, the differential Bianchi constraint is $$\sum_s I_s Z_{sr}=0\qquad(r=1,2,3).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-curvature-blocks"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-curvature-blocks</summary>

*Proof.* The first Bianchi identity says that the traces of the two diagonal blocks of the curvature operator agree. Their sum is half the scalar curvature, hence is $6$. Contraction identifies the off-diagonal block with the trace-free Ricci tensor: its nine entries give the coefficients in the independent symmetric trace-free matrices $I_sJ_t$. Thus the Einstein condition makes this block zero, and each diagonal block has trace $3$. Expanding in the orthonormal two-form bases gives the displayed curvature tensor.

A unit simple two-form has self-dual and anti-self-dual parts of norm $1/\sqrt2$. Conversely, a unit two-form whose two parts have these norms has exterior square zero, and is simple: in its orthogonal normal form $\alpha=a\,e^1\wedge e^2+b\,e^3\wedge e^4$, the condition $\alpha\wedge\alpha=0$ is $ab=0$. Therefore all pairs of plus and minus parts of norm $1/\sqrt2$ occur. The smallest sectional curvature is $1-(x+y)/2$, proving the equivalence.

For the norm comparison, write the eigenvalues of $T$ as $-x,a,x-a$. Minimality of $-x$ gives $-x\le a\le2x$, and hence $$\frac32x^2
 \le x^2+a^2+(x-a)^2
 \le6x^2.$$ Division by $6$ gives $v\le x\le2v$. The proof for $U$ is identical.

Contracting the differential Bianchi identity shows that the curvature tensor is divergence-free when Ricci is parallel. Project its last pair of indices onto $\Lambda^+$ or $\Lambda^-$; these projections are parallel. Subtracting the parallel scalar-curvature contribution proves divergence freedom of each Weyl block. In a quaternionic basis, the resulting constraint on $\nabla T$ is exactly the stated identity. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Algebra of the derivative representations

The differential Bianchi identity restricts $\nabla T$ to a proper subspace of all matrix-valued one-forms. To derive its Laplacian equation, we first describe this subspace and the curvature operators acting on it. The calculation uses only finite-dimensional tensor representations.

Let $\Sigma_s$ denote the infinitesimal action of $I_s$ on the plus indices of a tensor representation. On $\Lambda^+$ it is rotation about axis $s$ with speed $2$. A complexified irreducible representation has *spin $j$* when $$\sum_{s=1}^3\Sigma_s^2=-4j(j+1)\operatorname{Id}.$$ Pairs $(j,\ell)$ specify the spins of the two commuting rotation algebras. This notation describes associated tensor bundles of the oriented orthonormal frame bundle; it requires no spin structure.

<a id="stmt-lem-spin-algebra"></a>
<div id="lem:spin-algebra" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Representation rules). *A spin-$j$ representation has weights $-j,-j+1,\ldots,j$ for $\Sigma_3/(2i)$, each once, and $$H_j\otimes H_\ell
 \cong H_{j+\ell}\oplus H_{j+\ell-1}\oplus\cdots\oplus H_{|j-\ell|}.$$ In particular, the tangent, plus two-form, and plus trace-free symmetric-matrix representations have types $$\left(\frac12,\frac12\right),\qquad (1,0),\qquad (2,0).$$ The Casimir operators and the orthogonal projections onto these irreducible types are parallel bundle endomorphisms.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-spin-algebra"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-spin-algebra</summary>

*Proof.* The generators are skew-Hermitian and satisfy $[\Sigma_1,\Sigma_2]=2\Sigma_3$, cyclically. Diagonalize $\Sigma_3/(2i)$ and take the usual conjugate raising and lowering operators, which shift its weight by one. If a unit highest vector has weight $j$, commuting the raising and lowering operators gives the squared lowering coefficient at weight $j-r$ as $$(r+1)(2j-r).$$ Nonnegativity and termination of the chain imply that $2j$ is a nonnegative integer and give the listed weights and Casimir. Taking invariant orthogonal complements decomposes an arbitrary representation. Multiplying the weight lists, and successively removing the chain of highest remaining weight, proves the tensor product rule with multiplicity one. Commuting actions operate on the corresponding multiplicity spaces. The same chain description shows that an intertwiner between single irreducible copies is a scalar.

The actions on the tangent space and on $\Lambda^+$ give the first two types. The symmetric square of the spin-one representation is spin two plus the scalar trace, giving the third. Finally, these splittings are defined by invariant algebraic operators. The connection induced by the orthonormal frame bundle preserves every such operator. Thus the projections are parallel even though a chosen local frame, or a curvature eigenbasis, need not be parallel. ◻

</details>

Write $F=\operatorname{Sym}_0(\Lambda^+)$. For a symmetric trace-free matrix $A$, define the self-adjoint operator on $F$ $$B_F(A)S=\frac{AS+SA}{2}
         -\frac{\operatorname{tr}(AS)}3\operatorname{Id}.$$ It is characterized by $\langle B_F(A)S,S\rangle=\operatorname{tr}(AS^2)$. On any plus spin-$j$ representation with $j\ge1$, define $$Q_j(A)=\frac{\sum_{r,s}A_{rs}\Sigma_r\Sigma_s}{4j(2j-1)}.$$ For diagonal $A$ the numerator is $\sum_s a_s\Sigma_s^2$.

<a id="stmt-lem-quadratic-curvature-action"></a>
<div id="lem:quadratic-curvature-action" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Curvature-weighted operators). *If $A$ has smallest eigenvalue $-x$, then $$Q_j(A)\le \frac{x(j+1)}{2j-1}\operatorname{Id}.$$ Moreover, $Q_2(A)=B_F(A)$. The compression of $B_F(A)$, acting on an $F$ factor, to a highest-spin coupling of total plus spin $j$ is $Q_j(A)$, provided the operator acts trivially on any additional irreducible minus factor.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-quadratic-curvature-action"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-quadratic-curvature-action</summary>

*Proof.* In a diagonal basis, $a_s+x\ge0$ and every $\Sigma_s^2$ is nonpositive. Thus $$\sum_s a_s\Sigma_s^2
 \le -x\sum_s\Sigma_s^2
 =4xj(j+1)\operatorname{Id},$$ which proves the bound.

To identify $Q_2$, diagonalize $S=\operatorname{diag}(d_1,d_2,d_3)$. The speed-two matrix action gives $|\Sigma_sS|^2=8(d_j-d_k)^2$, with $s,j,k$ cyclic; these three derivatives are mutually orthogonal. Consequently, allowing $A$ to be nondiagonal in this basis, $$\begin{aligned}
 \langle Q_2(A)S,S\rangle
 &=-\frac13\sum_s A_{ss}(d_j-d_k)^2\\
 &=-\frac13\sum_s A_{ss}
       \bigl(2\operatorname{tr}(S^2)-3d_s^2\bigr)
 =\operatorname{tr}(AS^2).
 \end{aligned}$$ Here trace-freeness of both matrices gives the middle identity and removes the scalar term. Polarization proves the operator equality.

By Lemma <a href="#lem:spin-algebra" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:spin-algebra">4</a>, spin two occurs once in $\operatorname{End}(H_j)\cong H_j\otimes H_j$. Hence an equivariant operator family linear in $A$ is unique up to scalar. The same is true with an irreducible minus factor when the operators commute with its action. Test the scalar at $A=\operatorname{diag}(-1,-1,2)$ on a highest vector. At weight $m$, $$Q_j(A)=\frac{j(j+1)-3m^2}{j(2j-1)},$$ so its highest-weight eigenvalue is $-1$. The $F$ highest vector also has $B_F(A)$ eigenvalue $-1$. A product of highest vectors is the highest vector of the stretched coupling, proving the claimed compression. For the same reason, compression of a single generator from a spin-$\ell$ factor to a stretched spin-$j$ product is $\ell/j$ times the total generator. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-weyl-derivative-equations"></a>
<div id="prop:weyl-derivative-equations" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:1"></a>
**Proposition 6** (Differential equations for the Weyl blocks). *The tensor $Z=\nabla T$ belongs to the parallel subbundle $G$ of type $(5/2,1/2)$. On this bundle, put $B_{\mathrm{op}}=Q_{5/2}(T)$. Then $$\Delta T=6(T-N),\qquad
 N=(T^2)_0=T^2-2v^2\operatorname{Id},$$ and $$\Delta Z=(13-20B_{\mathrm{op}})Z.
 \tag{1}\label{eq:1}$$ Every statement has an exchanged counterpart for $U$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-weyl-derivative-equations"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-weyl-derivative-equations</summary>

*Proof.* The derivative space $T^*M\otimes F$ has types $(5/2,1/2)$ and $(3/2,1/2)$. The divergence takes values in $T^*M\otimes\Lambda^+$, whose plus spins are at most $3/2$. It is nonzero: a nonzero covector tensored with $\operatorname{diag}(1,-1,0)$ has nonzero divergence. It therefore vanishes on the first summand and is injective on the second, by irreducibility. Lemma <a href="#lem:curvature-blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:curvature-blocks">3</a> puts $Z$ in the first summand.

Let $$C=\sum_s I_s\otimes\Sigma_s$$ on $T^*M\otimes F$. Expansion of the combined Casimir gives $C=-4$ on $G$ and $C=6$ on its complement. In particular $P_G=(6-C)/10$ is a parallel orthogonal projector. The bundle curvature on $F$ is $$F_{ik}=\frac12\sum_s(1+t_s)I_{s,ik}\Sigma_s$$ at a point of diagonalization. Taking the covariant divergence of $CZ=-4Z$, using skewness of $I_s$ to take a commutator and $\sum_{ik}I_{s,ik}I_{r,ik}=4\delta_{sr}$, gives $$-4\Delta T=\sum_s(1+t_s)\Sigma_s^2T
            =-24T+24B_F(T)T=-24(T-N).$$

To differentiate this equation, note first that $\nabla N=2B_F(T)Z$. For each derivative index $j$, the covariant commutation formula reads $$\Delta Z_j
 =\nabla_j\Delta T+\nabla^i(F_{ij}T)
                    +F_{ij}\nabla^iT
                    +\operatorname{Ric}_{ja}Z_a.$$ The divergence of $F$ vanishes because it is the induced action of the divergence-free Riemann curvature. Thus the differentiated equation contributes $6Z-12B_F(T)Z$, the Ricci term contributes $3Z$, and the remaining bundle term is $-\sum_s(1+t_s)I_s\Sigma_sZ$. Its constant part is $-CZ=4Z$.

The left side already lies in $G$, since $P_G$ is parallel and $P_GZ=Z$. We may therefore compress the right side to $G$, although its individual summands need not preserve $G$. Lemma <a href="#lem:quadratic-curvature-action" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:quadratic-curvature-action">5</a> gives $P_GB_F(T)P_G=Q_{5/2}(T)$. On $G$, the total plus generators are $I_s+\Sigma_s$, so $$40Q_{5/2}(T)
 =P_G\sum_s t_s(I_s+\Sigma_s)^2P_G.$$ The $I_s^2$ term vanishes by $\sum_s t_s=0$, and the $\Sigma_s^2$ term compresses to $24Q_{5/2}(T)$. Hence $$P_G\sum_s t_s I_s\Sigma_sP_G=8Q_{5/2}(T).$$ The scalar contributions sum to $13$, while the two curvature contributions sum to $-20Q_{5/2}(T)$, proving <a href="#eq:1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:1">[eq:1]</a>. For a nondiagonal $T$ the same calculation uses the symmetric contraction $\sum_{r,s}T_{rs}(I_r+\Sigma_r)(I_s+\Sigma_s)$: its $I_rI_s$ part is zero by symmetry and trace-freeness. Thus the computation is tensorial and entails no differentiation of a moving eigenframe. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### The gradient parametrization and the norm equation

Write $$p=\nabla v,\qquad q=\nabla b,\qquad
 e=\frac{|\nabla T|^2}{6},\qquad
 \bar e=\frac{|\nabla U|^2}{6}.$$ The norms $v,b$ are Lipschitz. Their gradients are understood almost everywhere and vanish almost everywhere on the respective zero sets.

Where $v>0$, diagonalize $T$ and put, cyclically in $i,j,k$, $$t_i=vs_i,\qquad
 \widetilde s_i=\frac{s_j-s_k}{\sqrt3},\qquad
 \sigma=\frac16\sum_i s_i^3,\qquad
 \rho=\frac16\sum_i s_i^2\widetilde s_i.$$ Here $\sum s_i=0$ and $\sum s_i^2=6$. On the plane of triples with zero sum, the map $s\mapsto\widetilde s$ is a skew-adjoint orthogonal quarter-turn. Thus $$\sum_i\widetilde s_i=0,\qquad
 \sum_i\widetilde s_i^2=6,\qquad
 \sum_i s_i\widetilde s_i=0,\qquad
 s_i^2+\widetilde s_i^2=4.$$ For later use, these identities imply $$\sigma^2+\rho^2=1,\qquad
 \frac{N}{v^2}
   =\operatorname{diag}(\sigma s_i+\rho\widetilde s_i),
 \qquad
 \frac16\sum_i\widetilde s_i^3=-\rho.$$ Indeed, the trace-free triple $n_i=s_i^2-2$ has squared norm $6$: use $\sum_i s_i^4=(\sum_i s_i^2)^2/2=18$. Its coefficients in the orthogonal basis $s,\widetilde s$ are $\sigma,\rho$, proving the first two assertions. The last follows by factoring the alternating cubic $\sum_i s_i^2(s_j-s_k)$. If $r=x/v$, the other two eigenvalues besides $-r$ have sum $r$ and product $r^2-3$, so $$1\le r\le2,\qquad
 \sigma=\frac{3r-r^3}{2}.$$ The normalized triples are used only where the corresponding block is nonzero. On $\{v=0\}$ set $\sigma=\rho=0$. Since $|\sigma|,|\rho|\le1$, their products with any positive power of $v$ have continuous zero extensions; in particular $\sigma v^3=\operatorname{tr}(T^3)/6$ is smooth everywhere. Bars denote the analogous quantities for $U$.

The next formula gives, in particular, the refined Kato inequality $|\nabla T|^2\ge(5/3)|\nabla|T||^2$ for a half-Weyl block. Gursky–LeBrun derive this estimate from the same irreducible Bianchi gradient constraint <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[GurskyLeBrun1999](#ref-GurskyLeBrun1999), Lemma 4, Equations (10)–(12)]; Calderbank–Gauduchon–Herzlich give its general representation-theoretic setting <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[CalderbankGauduchonHerzlich2000](#ref-CalderbankGauduchonHerzlich2000), Equation (7.1)]. The explicit parametrization below, and the weak norm equation following it, are those of <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[OpenAI2026P](#ref-OpenAI2026P), Lemma 2.5 and Proposition 2.6]. We retain all three vector parameters: the excess over the norm-gradient energy will be needed in the coupled estimate.

<a id="stmt-lem-gradient-parametrization"></a>
<div id="lem:gradient-parametrization" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:2"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Parametrization of the Bianchi gradient space). *There are unique vectors $k,l$ such that $$\begin{gathered}
 Z_{ii}=s_i p-\sqrt3\,\widetilde s_i k,\qquad
 Z_{jk}=-I_i\left(l+s_i k+\frac{\widetilde s_i}{\sqrt3}p\right),
 \qquad (i,j,k)\ \text{cyclic},\\
 e=\frac53|p|^2+5|k|^2+|l|^2.
 \end{gathered}
 \tag{2}\label{eq:2}$$ More generally, for any fixed normalized trace-free diagonal triple $s$, the same formulas parametrize the entire algebraic space $G$ by three arbitrary real four-vectors.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-gradient-parametrization"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-gradient-parametrization</summary>

*Proof.* Write $D_i=Z_{ii}$ and $a_i=I_iZ_{jk}$. Quaternion multiplication turns the three divergence equations into $D_i+a_j-a_k=0$. Trace-freeness and the orthogonal basis $s,\widetilde s$ give uniquely $$p=\frac16\sum_i s_iD_i,\qquad
 k=-\frac1{6\sqrt3}\sum_i\widetilde s_iD_i,\qquad
 D_i=s_ip-\sqrt3\,\widetilde s_i k.$$ Since $s_j-s_k=\sqrt3\,\widetilde s_i$ and $\widetilde s_j-\widetilde s_k=-\sqrt3\,s_i$, the vectors $s_i k+\widetilde s_i p/\sqrt3$ have exactly the differences required of the $a_i$. Their difference from $a_i$ is therefore a common vector $l$, proving the formulas and their converse. This also establishes their full algebraic scope.

Orthogonality of $s,\widetilde s$ gives $$\sum_i|Z_{ii}|^2=6|p|^2+18|k|^2,\qquad
 2\sum_{j<k}|Z_{jk}|^2=4|p|^2+12|k|^2+6|l|^2.$$ The factor two in the second sum is the Hilbert–Schmidt multiplicity of off-diagonal entries. Division by six proves the norm identity. For $Z=\nabla T$, $\nabla|T|^2=2v\sum_i s_iZ_{ii}=12vp$; comparison with $|T|^2=6v^2$ identifies the parameter $p$ with $\nabla v$. ◻

</details>

The pointwise norm equation alone is insufficient for integration: a Weyl block may vanish. We next prove that its regularized Laplacians converge to an actual integrable density, so no measure supported on the zero set is lost.

<a id="stmt-prop-weak-norm-equation"></a>
<div id="prop:weak-norm-equation" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:3"></a>
**Proposition 8** (Weak norm equation, including the zero set). *The function $e/v$, defined to be zero on $\{v=0\}$, belongs to $L^1(M)$, and $$\Delta v=6v(1-\sigma v)+\frac{e-|p|^2}{v}
 \tag{3}\label{eq:3}$$ holds as an equality of distributions with an $L^1$ density. The quotient is zero almost everywhere on the zero set. This identity may be tested against bounded Lipschitz functions. The corresponding assertions hold for $b$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-weak-norm-equation"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-weak-norm-equation</summary>

*Proof.* On $\{v>0\}$, pair the equation for $\Delta T$ with $T$ to obtain $$\Delta(v^2)=12(v^2-\sigma v^3)+2e.$$ This is globally an identity of smooth functions. Put $v_\varepsilon=\sqrt{v^2+\varepsilon^2}$. Direct differentiation gives $$\Delta v_\varepsilon
 =\frac{6(v^2-\sigma v^3)}{v_\varepsilon}
   +q_\varepsilon,\qquad
 q_\varepsilon=\frac e{v_\varepsilon}
             -\frac{v^2|p|^2}{v_\varepsilon^3}.$$ The refined derivative estimate in <a href="#eq:2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:2">[eq:2]</a> yields $$\frac25\frac e{v_\varepsilon}
 \le q_\varepsilon\le\frac e{v_\varepsilon}.$$ These assertions hold almost everywhere, including the zero set: every smooth component of $T$ has derivative zero almost everywhere on its zero set, hence $Z=0$ almost everywhere on $\{T=0\}$. One can see this elementary fact at density points of a level set; a nonzero derivative would instead make that scalar level set a local hypersurface.

The first term in $\Delta v_\varepsilon$ is uniformly bounded in absolute value by $6(v+v^2)$. Its integral is the negative of the integral of $q_\varepsilon$. Consequently $\mathbf E(e/v_\varepsilon)$ is uniformly bounded. Monotone convergence proves the asserted $L^1$ bound for $e/v$. Off the zero set, $q_\varepsilon$ is dominated by $e/v$ and tends to $(e-|p|^2)/v$; on the zero set both are zero almost everywhere. Dominated convergence therefore gives an $L^1$ limit, rather than only a weak limit of measures. The zeroth-order term converges in $L^1$ as well. Since $v_\varepsilon\to v$ uniformly, their Laplacians converge distributionally, proving <a href="#eq:3" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:3">[eq:3]</a> without a residual singular measure.

Finally, approximate a bounded Lipschitz test function uniformly and in $H^1$ by smooth functions, using local mollification and a partition of unity. Uniform convergence controls its pairing with the $L^1$ right side, and $H^1$ convergence controls its gradient pairing with $\nabla v$. This proves the asserted testing rule. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:analytic"></a>
### Scalar estimates and the characteristic identities

This section supplies two kinds of global information: analytic bounds for the sizes of the Weyl blocks, and integer identities relating their second moments to volume. The former will control derivatives in the coupled inequality; the latter will restrict the possible imbalance between the two blocks.

Throughout, $M$ is the closed, simply connected, oriented Einstein four-manifold obtained in the introduction, with $\operatorname{Ric}=3g$ and nonnegative sectional curvature. We adapt the scalar estimates of <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[OpenAI2026P](#ref-OpenAI2026P), Propositions 3.1–3.4]. The weighted variance proof uses the closed bound $x+y\le2$; the norm-gap proof uses only the Einstein equation and handles zeros directly.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### A strengthened scalar variance estimate

<a id="stmt-prop-weighted-variance"></a>
<div id="prop:weighted-variance" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:4"></a>
**Proposition 9** (Weighted variance). *For every real $a\in H^1(M)$, $$\operatorname{Var}(a)
 :=\mathbf E\bigl[(a-\mathbf E a)^2\bigr]
 \le\mathbf E\frac{|\nabla a|^2}{K},
 \qquad K=4+v^2+b^2.
 \tag{4}\label{eq:4}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-weighted-variance"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-weighted-variance</summary>

*Proof.* First let $\phi$ be smooth and write $S=\operatorname{Hess}_0\phi$. The integrated Bochner identity and $\operatorname{Ric}=3g$ give $$\mathbf E\bigl[(\Delta\phi)^2\bigr]
 =\mathbf E\left(4|\nabla\phi|^2+\frac43|S|^2\right).$$ Define operators on covariant two-tensors by $$(T_D)_{ikjl}=\frac12\sum_s t_s I_{s,ik}I_{s,jl},
 \qquad
 (U_D)_{ikjl}=\frac12\sum_s u_s J_{s,ik}J_{s,jl},$$ where $ij$ is the row index and $kl$ is the column index. These tensorial definitions may be evaluated after pointwise diagonalization. They annihilate metric trace. On the symmetric trace-free tensors, the nine matrices $I_iJ_j$ form an orthogonal basis, with $T_D$ and $U_D$ eigenvalues $t_i$ and $u_j$. For example, $T_D$ acts by $-\frac12\sum_s t_s I_s(\,\cdot\,)I_s$; the quaternion relations give the eigenvalue $t_i$ on $I_iJ_j$. Thus $$T_D+U_D\le 2(x+y)\operatorname{Id}\le4\operatorname{Id}$$ on this space.

The integrated curvature contraction has a useful sign and a precise coefficient. Since $T_D$ annihilates trace, replace $S$ by $\operatorname{Hess}\phi$ in its quadratic form. Integrate the derivative $k$, use $\nabla_k(T_D)_{ikjl}=0$, and use skewness in $i,k$ to obtain $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbf E\langle T_DS,S\rangle
 &=-\mathbf E (T_D)_{ikjl}\phi_l\nabla_k\nabla_i\phi_j\\
 &=\frac12\mathbf E (T_D)_{ikjl}R_{ikja}\phi_a\phi_l.
 \end{aligned}$$ In this contraction the minus curvature block is orthogonal to the plus block. Substituting their expansions gives $$\frac12\sum_{ikj}(T_D)_{ikjl}R_{ikja}
 =\frac12\sum_s t_s(1+t_s)
                 \sum_j I_{s,jl}I_{s,ja}
 =3v^2\delta_{la}.$$ The final equality uses $\sum t_s=0$ and $\sum t_s^2=6v^2$. Consequently $$\mathbf E\langle(T_D+U_D)S,S\rangle
 =3\mathbf E\bigl[(v^2+b^2)|\nabla\phi|^2\bigr].$$ The pointwise upper operator bound, inserted into the positive $|S|^2$ term of the Bochner identity, now yields $$\mathbf E\bigl[(\Delta\phi)^2\bigr]\ge\mathbf E K|\nabla\phi|^2.$$

For smooth $a$, the Poisson equation on a closed connected manifold gives a unique smooth zero-mean solution of $\Delta\phi=a-\mathbf E a$. Integration by parts and Cauchy–Schwarz with the pointwise weight $K$ give $$\begin{aligned}
 \operatorname{Var}(a)
 &=-\mathbf E\langle\nabla a,\nabla\phi\rangle\\
 &\le
 \left(\mathbf E\frac{|\nabla a|^2}{K}\right)^{1/2}
 \left(\mathbf E K|\nabla\phi|^2\right)^{1/2}\\
 &\le
 \left(\mathbf E\frac{|\nabla a|^2}{K}\right)^{1/2}
 \operatorname{Var}(a)^{1/2}.
 \end{aligned}$$ Division proves the assertion when the variance is nonzero; the zero case is immediate. Smooth approximation in $H^1$ completes the proof: $K$ is bounded above and below by positive constants, so the weighted energy also converges. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### The sharp scalar inequality and the Weyl gap

The following is the four-dimensional Einstein case of Ilias’s sharp positive-Ricci Sobolev inequality <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Ilias](#ref-Ilias), Theorem 3]; see also <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[OpenAI2026P](#ref-OpenAI2026P), Proposition 3.2] for this normalization. The use of normalized volume makes its constants independent of $\operatorname{Vol}(M)$.

<a id="stmt-prop-sharp-sobolev"></a>
<div id="prop:sharp-sobolev" class="proposition">

**Proposition 10** (Normalized Sobolev inequality). *For every real $f\in H^1(M)$, $$\bigl(\mathbf E|f|^4\bigr)^{1/2}
 \le\mathbf E\left(f^2+\frac12|\nabla f|^2\right).$$*

</div>

The next proposition is the Weyl-norm gap established in Gursky–LeBrun <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[GurskyLeBrun1999](#ref-GurskyLeBrun1999), Theorem 1]; we give the derivation in our normalization, following <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[OpenAI2026P](#ref-OpenAI2026P), Proposition 3.3]. The regularization is included because neither Weyl norm is assumed to be everywhere positive.

<a id="stmt-prop-weyl-gap"></a>
<div id="prop:weyl-gap" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:5"></a>
**Proposition 11** (Weyl-norm gap). *For either Weyl block, $$v\equiv0\qquad\text{or}\qquad \mathbf E v^2\ge1.
 \tag{5}\label{eq:5}$$ If $T\not\equiv0$ and $\mathbf E v^2=1$, then $\nabla T=0$ and $v=\sigma=1$. The corresponding statements hold for $U$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-weyl-gap"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-weyl-gap</summary>

*Proof.* Put $A_\varepsilon=v^2+\varepsilon^2$ and $f_\varepsilon=A_\varepsilon^{1/6}$. These are smooth functions. The equation for $\Delta(v^2)$ and <a href="#eq:2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:2">[eq:2]</a> give $$\begin{aligned}
 \Delta f_\varepsilon
 =A_\varepsilon^{-5/6}
 \left[
  2(v^2-\sigma v^3)+\frac13e
       -\frac59\frac{v^2}{A_\varepsilon}|p|^2
 \right]
 \ge 2A_\varepsilon^{-5/6}(v^2-v^3).
 \end{aligned}$$ Indeed, $\sigma\le1$, $v^2/A_\varepsilon\le1$, and $e\ge5|p|^2/3$. Multiply by $f_\varepsilon$ and integrate: $$\mathbf E|\nabla f_\varepsilon|^2
 \le 2\mathbf E\frac{v^3-v^2}{A_\varepsilon^{2/3}}
 \le 2\mathbf E v^{5/3}.$$ Their $L^2$ norms are uniformly bounded as well. Thus $f_\varepsilon$ is bounded in $H^1$, and its uniform limit $f=v^{1/3}$ belongs to $H^1$ by weak compactness. This bound is obtained directly from the regularized equation; it requires no positive lower bound on $v$.

Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:sharp-sobolev" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:sharp-sobolev">10</a> to $f_\varepsilon$ and use the preceding energy bound: $$\begin{aligned}
 \bigl(\mathbf E f_\varepsilon^4\bigr)^{1/2}
 &\le \mathbf E\left[
       A_\varepsilon^{1/3}
       +\frac{v^3-v^2}{A_\varepsilon^{2/3}}\right]\\
 &=\mathbf E\frac{\varepsilon^2+v^3}{A_\varepsilon^{2/3}}.
 \end{aligned}$$ The term $\varepsilon^2/A_\varepsilon^{2/3}$ is at most $\varepsilon^{2/3}$, while $v^3/A_\varepsilon^{2/3}\le v^{5/3}$. Dominated convergence therefore gives $$\bigl(\mathbf E f^4\bigr)^{1/2}
 \le\mathbf E(f^2v)
 \le\bigl(\mathbf E f^4\bigr)^{1/2}
       \bigl(\mathbf E v^2\bigr)^{1/2}.$$ When $T\not\equiv0$, the common factor is positive, proving <a href="#eq:5" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:5">[eq:5]</a>.

If $\mathbf E v^2=1$, equality holds in Cauchy–Schwarz. Thus $v=\alpha f^2$ almost everywhere for a constant $\alpha>0$. It follows that $v$ takes only the values $0$ and $\alpha^3$ almost everywhere, and hence everywhere by continuity. Connectedness and $T\not\equiv0$ force the positive constant value on all of $M$. The moment condition gives $v=1$. Equation <a href="#eq:3" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:3">[eq:3]</a> then becomes $0=6(1-\sigma)+e$. Both terms are nonnegative, so $\sigma=1$ and $e=0$. Smoothness gives $\nabla T=0$ everywhere. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### The Euler and signature formulas

<a id="stmt-prop-characteristic-identities"></a>
<div id="prop:characteristic-identities" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:6"></a>
**Proposition 12** (Characteristic identities). *Let $n_+$ and $n_-$ be the finite nonnegative integer dimensions of the spaces of harmonic self-dual and anti-self-dual two-forms. The Euler characteristic and signature of $M$ are, respectively, $$\chi=2+n_++n_-,\qquad \tau=n_+-n_-.$$ Then $$\begin{aligned}
 8\pi^2\chi
   &=6\operatorname{Vol}(M)
        \bigl(1+\mathbf E(v^2+b^2)\bigr),\\
 12\pi^2\tau
   &=6\operatorname{Vol}(M)\,\mathbf E(v^2-b^2).
 \end{aligned}
 \tag{6}\label{eq:6}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-characteristic-identities"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-characteristic-identities</summary>

*Proof.* Simple connectivity gives $b_1(M)=0$, and Poincaré duality gives $b_3(M)=0$. Hodge theory identifies $b_2=n_++n_-$ and the signature with $n_+-n_-$, proving the first two identities. The Chern–Gauss–Bonnet and signature formulas for an Einstein four-manifold are $$8\pi^2\chi=\int_M\left(|T|^2+|U|^2+\frac{R^2}{24}\right)
                 \,d\operatorname{vol},\qquad
