 Z_\varepsilon=0,\qquad \bar Z_\varepsilon=\bar Z.$$ These are pointwise algebraic data; no metric deformation is involved. Their norms and least-eigenvalue variables satisfy $$v_\varepsilon=\varepsilon,\qquad
 b_\varepsilon=(1-\varepsilon)b>0,\qquad
 x_\varepsilon+y_\varepsilon
   =\varepsilon+(1-\varepsilon)y\le2-\varepsilon<2.$$ The norm inequality $v_\varepsilon+b_\varepsilon\le
x_\varepsilon+y_\varepsilon$ gives the other required domain condition. The Bianchi gradient space is independent of the spectra, so the fixed tensor $\bar Z$ remains admissible. Moreover, the induced minus norm derivative is unchanged: $$(q_\varepsilon)_i
 =\frac{\langle(1-\varepsilon)U,\bar Z_i\rangle}
         {6(1-\varepsilon)b}=q_i.$$ Set all plus derivative parameters equal to zero. Then every numerator divided by $v_\varepsilon$ vanishes identically before passing to the limit. The minus denominators tend to positive values, and every remaining term tends to its prescribed value at the original data. Thus each of the three estimates passes to this zero set. In particular, the quartic target tends to $-c|q|^4/b$ and all cross terms with $p$ vanish. The same argument with the labels exchanged treats $\{b=0,\ v>0\}$. On $\{v=b=0\}$ both derivative tensors vanish almost everywhere; use two small axial spectra and zero derivative tensors. Products such as $\sigma v^2$ tend to zero because $|\sigma|\le1$.

This proves the pointwise estimates almost everywhere, with the quotient convention of <a href="#eq:3" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:3">[eq:3]</a>. The quartic quotients are bounded in absolute value by a constant times $e/v+\bar e/b$, and hence are integrable. Proposition <a href="#prop:coupling-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:coupling-estimates">28</a> follows once the explicit polynomial inequalities in Appendix <a href="#sec:certificates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:certificates">8</a> are established.

<a id="sec:classification"></a>
### The equality case and the parallel splitting

The preceding estimates have nonnegative integral and a nonpositive pointwise upper bound. We now determine what their equality means. The argument first makes the two norms constant; the norm equations then make the Weyl tensors parallel. These are separate steps.

<a id="stmt-prop-constant-norms"></a>
<div id="prop:constant-norms" class="proposition">

**Proposition 29** (Constancy of the two Weyl norms). *On a closed, simply connected, oriented four-manifold with $\operatorname{Ric}=3g$, nonnegative sectional curvature, and neither Weyl block identically zero, one has $v=b=1$ everywhere.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-constant-norms"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-constant-norms</summary>

*Proof.* Proposition <a href="#prop:balanced-moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:balanced-moments">20</a> gives $\mathbf E v^2=\mathbf E b^2$, so Proposition <a href="#prop:coupling-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:coupling-estimates">28</a> supplies an integrable function $\mathcal I$ with $\mathbf E\mathcal I\ge0$ and $$\mathcal I\le-cj+2\Theta r-\frac{\Theta^2}{c},\qquad
 c\ge\frac1{10},$$ where $$r=R_v|p|^2+R_b|q|^2,\quad
 j=(|p|^2+|q|^2)\left(\frac{|p|^2}{v}+\frac{|q|^2}{b}\right),
 \quad p=dv,\quad q=db.$$ The zero-set conventions of Proposition <a href="#prop:weak-norm-equation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:weak-norm-equation">8</a> apply. The gradients are bounded, and the derivative quotients are integrable, so $j$ is integrable as well.

For $v>0$ the identity $$1+6v+v^2-4\sqrt v(1+v)=(\sqrt v-1)^4$$ gives $R_v\le1/\sqrt v$, with equality precisely when $v=1$. Consequently Cauchy–Schwarz gives $$r\le\frac{|p|^2}{\sqrt v}+\frac{|q|^2}{\sqrt b}\le\sqrt j.$$ A summand with zero numerator and zero denominator is read as zero. The first inequality is strict almost everywhere on the set where $p$ or $q$ is nonzero. To see this, recall that a Lipschitz function has zero differential almost everywhere on each of its level sets: at a differentiability and density point of a level set, a nonzero differential would contradict density. Outside a null set, a nonzero $p$ thus has $v>0$ and $v\ne1$, and the analogous statement holds for $q$. At least one summand is then strictly smaller.

Completing the square now gives $$\mathcal I\le-\frac{(\Theta-cr)^2}{c}-c(j-r^2)\le0.$$ The right side is strictly negative almost everywhere where either norm gradient is nonzero. Since $\mathbf E\mathcal I\ge0$, that set has measure zero. Both weak gradients vanish; connectedness makes the Lipschitz functions $v,b$ constant. Neither constant is zero by hypothesis. The gaps in Proposition <a href="#prop:weyl-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:weyl-gap">11</a> give $v,b\ge1$, while $v+b\le2$. Thus both constants equal $1$. ◻

</details>

The strictness in this argument uses the level sets of the Weyl norms. It does not require the quadratic form in the Hessian identity to be positive definite. This is why the same coupling estimates determine the boundary geometry.

<a id="stmt-prop-parallel-blocks"></a>
<div id="prop:parallel-blocks" class="proposition">

**Proposition 30** (Parallel Weyl blocks). *Both $T$ and $U$ are parallel, with eigenvalues $(2,-1,-1)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-parallel-blocks"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-parallel-blocks</summary>

*Proof.* Since $v=1$ and $p=0$, the weak norm Equation <a href="#eq:3" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:3">[eq:3]</a> becomes $$0=6(1-\sigma)+e.$$ Here $\sigma\le1$ and $e=|\nabla T|^2/6\ge0$. Both terms vanish, so $\sigma=1$ and $\nabla T=0$; smoothness gives these identities everywhere. The exchanged equation gives the same conclusion for $U$. The trace, squared norm, and cubic trace of each block are respectively $0,6,6$. Its characteristic polynomial is therefore $\lambda^3-3\lambda-2=(\lambda-2)(\lambda+1)^2$, as claimed. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-parallel-blocks-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-parallel-blocks</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* We have reduced to the complete simply connected oriented cover. By Proposition <a href="#prop:parallel-blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:parallel-blocks">30</a>, the simple eigenspaces of $T$ and $U$ are parallel real line subbundles of $\Lambda^+$ and $\Lambda^-$. A real line bundle over a simply connected manifold is trivial. Choose global unit sections $\omega_+$ and $\omega_-$ of these lines. Their covariant derivatives lie in the respective lines and are orthogonal to the unit sections, so the sections are parallel.

Viewed as skew endomorphisms, $I=\sqrt2\,\omega_+$ and $J=\sqrt2\,\omega_-$ are orthogonal complex structures of opposite orientations. The two rotation algebras commute, so $IJ=JI$. The product $A=IJ$ is parallel and satisfies $$A^*=A,\qquad A^2=\operatorname{Id},\qquad
 \operatorname{tr}A=0.$$ The last equality follows from the orthogonality of self-dual and anti-self-dual two-forms. Thus the $+1$ and $-1$ eigenspaces of $A$ are orthogonal parallel rank-two distributions.

The de Rham decomposition theorem <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[deRham1952](#ref-deRham1952)] gives a global Riemannian product of complete simply connected surfaces. The Ricci tensor of a product restricts to the Ricci tensor of each factor, so each surface has $\operatorname{Ric}=3g$, equivalently Gaussian curvature $3$. A complete simply connected surface of constant curvature $3$ is the round sphere of radius $1/\sqrt3$. This identifies the universal Riemannian cover as the asserted product. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-parallel-blocks-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-parallel-blocks</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:nonnegative-classification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:nonnegative-classification">2</a>.* Pass to the compact, simply connected, oriented universal Riemannian cover, as in Section <a href="#sec:introduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:introduction">1</a>. If one Weyl block vanishes identically, the classical half-conformally-flat classification recalled there gives the standard sphere or complex projective plane. Otherwise Propositions <a href="#prop:constant-norms" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:constant-norms">29</a> and <a href="#prop:parallel-blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:parallel-blocks">30</a> give parallel Weyl blocks with spectrum $(2,-1,-1)$. The parallel splitting just proved then gives the product of two round spheres. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:certificates"></a>
### Exact polynomial certificates

This appendix proves the finite polynomial inequalities used in Section <a href="#sec:coupling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:coupling">6</a>. The coefficients are rational throughout. The certificates are those of <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[OpenAI2026P](#ref-OpenAI2026P), Section 7]. We give the conversion formulas, domains, and coefficient bounds so that each sign follows from a finite polynomial expansion. The method uses the nonnegative Bernstein basis on intervals and simplices; see Boudaoud–Caruso–Roy <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[BoudaoudCarusoRoy2008](#ref-BoudaoudCarusoRoy2008)] and Roy <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[Roy2007](#ref-Roy2007), Sections 1 and 4]. We prove the conversion formulas and their matrix-valued consequence here. Positivity is verified for these specific polynomials, with their boundary zeros retained. The accompanying source package contains a standard-library Python checker at . From the source package’s root, the exact coefficient calculations can be reproduced with

    python -B verification/verify.py --check

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### Bernstein coefficients on intervals and triangles

<a id="stmt-lem-bernstein-conversion"></a>
<div id="lem:bernstein-conversion" class="lemma">

**Lemma 31** (Bernstein conversion). *Let $p$ be a polynomial of degree at most $N$ on an interval $[r,s]$, and write $$p(r+(s-r)z)=\sum_{j=0}^N a_jz^j.$$ Its degree-$N$ Bernstein expansion is $$p(r+(s-r)z)=\sum_{i=0}^N h_i\binom Ni z^i(1-z)^{N-i},
 \qquad
 h_i=\sum_{j=0}^i a_j\frac{\binom ij}{\binom Nj}.$$ In particular, $h_i\ge\eta$ for all $i$ implies $p\ge\eta$ on $[r,s]$.*

*For a polynomial on a triangle with ordered vertices $p_\ell=(r_\ell,s_\ell)$, set $$V=r_3+(r_1-r_3)z+(r_2-r_3)w,\qquad
 W=s_3+(s_1-s_3)z+(s_2-s_3)w.$$ If $P(z,w)=p(V,W)=\sum_{d,e}P_{de}z^dw^e$ has degree at most $N$, then its normalized degree-$N$ triangular coefficients are $$[p]_{ij}=s_{ij}(P):=
 \sum_{d=0}^i\sum_{e=0}^j
 \frac{\binom id\binom je}
      {\binom N{d+e}\binom{d+e}d}\,P_{de},
 \qquad i,j\ge0,\quad i+j\le N.$$ On $z,w\ge0$, $z+w\le1$, the polynomial is the sum of these coefficients times the nonnegative basis functions $$\frac{N!}{i!j!(N-i-j)!}
       z^iw^j(1-z-w)^{N-i-j}.$$ These basis functions sum to one. The same expansion applies entrywise to a symmetric polynomial matrix: positive semidefiniteness of every coefficient matrix implies positive semidefiniteness throughout the triangle.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-bernstein-conversion"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-bernstein-conversion</summary>

*Proof.* For an interval, expand $z^j=z^j(z+(1-z))^{N-j}$ and compare coefficients in the Bernstein basis. On a triangle, expand $$z^dw^e=z^dw^e(z+w+(1-z-w))^{N-d-e}.$$ This gives the displayed factors after dividing each coefficient by the corresponding multinomial coefficient. Equivalently, substitute $$(v,b)=\frac{u_1p_1+u_2p_2+u_3p_3}{u_1+u_2+u_3}$$ and multiply by $(u_1+u_2+u_3)^N$. The normalized coefficient of $u_1^iu_2^ju_3^{N-i-j}$ is $[p]_{ij}$. The positivity statements follow from the nonnegative partition of unity, also for matrix quadratic forms. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### The quartic scalar certificates

<a id="stmt-lem-scalar-certificates"></a>
<div id="lem:scalar-certificates" class="lemma">

**Lemma 32** (Scalar certificates). *For the polynomials in Section <a href="#sec:coupling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:coupling">6</a>, the following bounds hold: $$\begin{gathered}
 C_0>0,\qquad
 4C_0\left((h-A_0)(2-v)+\frac25hv\right)
       -(2-v)(2A_0+L_0)^2>0\quad(0\le v\le2),\\
 G,H>0\quad(0\le u\le1),\qquad
 Z_*\le0\quad(0\le u\le1/16),\\
 Z_*^2\le64A^2u^2GH\quad(1/16\le u\le1).
\end{gathered}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-scalar-certificates"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-scalar-certificates</summary>

*Proof.* Apply the interval formula of Lemma <a href="#lem:bernstein-conversion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:bernstein-conversion">31</a> in the degrees and on the intervals indicated below. Every coefficient has at least the displayed positive lower bound. In the first two rows the bound applies on each of the two intervals separately.

<div class="center">

| Polynomial                    | Degree |    Interval     | Lower bound |
|:------------------------------|:------:|:---------------:|:-----------:|
| $C_0$                         |  $2$   | $[0,1],\ [1,2]$ |    $1/2$    |
| $4C_0((h-A_0)(2-v)+\frac25hv) 
  -(2-v)(2A_0+L_0)^2$           |  $5$   | $[0,1],\ [1,2]$ |    $1/2$    |
| $G$                           |  $4$   |     $[0,1]$     |   $9/10$    |
| $H$                           |  $4$   |     $[0,1]$     |    $1/4$    |
| $-Z_*$                        |  $8$   |   $[0,1/16]$    |  $13/100$   |
| $64A^2u^2GH-Z_*^2$            |  $16$  |   $[1/16,1]$    |  $12/100$   |

</div>

For the first two rows the coefficients are in fact strictly greater than $1/2$. For example, their respective minima on the two intervals are $$\left(\frac{3971}{3200},\frac{1063}{1600}\right),
 \qquad
 \left(\frac{101027}{20000},\frac{283729}{400000}\right).$$ All entries in this table are obtained by substituting the displayed formulas for $A_0,L_0,C_0$ and for $A,G,H,L,Z_*$ into the coefficient sum in Lemma <a href="#lem:bernstein-conversion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:bernstein-conversion">31</a>. Thus each bound is an inequality between rational coefficients. The lemma proves the required signs. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
#### The two matrix certificates

For the quadratic inequality <a href="#eq:22" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:22">[eq:22]</a>, introduce $$\begin{aligned}
 r_v&=1+6v+v^2,\qquad r_b=1+6b+b^2,
       \qquad \alpha=\frac54,\\
 H_v&=8vK\Theta(1+v)
       -r_v\left\{vD+K\left[v(X_v+\partial_v\kappa)
                                      +\frac{2\kappa}{3}\right]\right\},\\
 H_b&=H_v(b,v),\\
 S_*&=4(\alpha+\alpha^{-1})vb
       +\frac{\alpha b(1-v)^2+\alpha^{-1}v(1-b)^2}{2}.
\end{aligned}$$ The diagonal differences in <a href="#eq:22" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:22">[eq:22]</a> are $H_v/(vKr_v)$ and $H_b/(bKr_b)$. Since $r_v=8v+(1-v)^2$ and $r_b=8b+(1-b)^2$, weighted arithmetic–geometric mean gives $$\sqrt{vb r_vr_b}
       \le\frac{\alpha b r_v+\alpha^{-1}v r_b}{2}=S_*.$$ It is therefore sufficient to prove $$\begin{pmatrix}H_v&S_*KJ\\S_*KJ&H_b\end{pmatrix}\succeq0.$$ Indeed its diagonal entries are nonnegative and its determinant gives $H_vH_b\ge S_*^2K^2J^2\ge vb r_vr_bK^2J^2$. Dividing by the positive denominators proves that the matrix of differences in <a href="#eq:22" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:22">[eq:22]</a> is positive semidefinite. This argument uses $J^2$ and imposes no sign condition on $J$.

For a symmetric $2\times2$ matrix, record its entries in the order $(11,22,12)$ as a triple $(r,h',s')$. In this notation the two triples to be certified are $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal A={}&P_0\bigl(v(K_1,K_2,K_{12})-\kappa(5,1,0)\bigr)
       -2v^2(P_k^2,P_l^2,P_kP_l),\qquad \sigma=1,\\
 \mathcal B={}&(H_v,H_b,S_*KJ).
\end{aligned}$$ The first triple is precisely the matrix in <a href="#eq:21" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:21">[eq:21]</a> at $\sigma=1$.

<a id="stmt-lem-matrix-certificates"></a>
<div id="lem:matrix-certificates" class="lemma">

**Lemma 33** (Matrix and boundary certificates). *The symmetric matrix represented by $\mathcal A$ is positive semidefinite on $$\mathcal D=\{(v,b):v,b\ge0,\ v+b\le2\}.$$ The symmetric matrix represented by $\mathcal B$ is positive semidefinite on $$\mathcal D_+=\{(v,b)\in\mathcal D:v\ge b\}.$$ On $\mathcal D_+$ one also has $$L_v\ge0,\qquad F+12c\delta^2L_b\ge0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-matrix-certificates"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-matrix-certificates</summary>

*Proof.* Use the vertices $$\begin{gathered}
 O=(0,0),\quad S=(1,0),\quad C=(1,1),\quad
 U=(2,0),\quad W_0=(0,2),\\
 A=(1/2,1/2),\quad B=(3/2,1/2),\quad E=(1,1/2).
\end{gathered}$$ A three-letter word specifies the vertices of an ordered triangle. The triangles $OSC$, $OCW_0$, and $SCU$ cover $\mathcal D$. The triangles $OAS$, $ASE$, $ACE$, $SBE$, $CBE$, and $SUB$ cover $\mathcal D_+$; the latter is also the single triangle $OCU$, or the union $OSC\cup SCU$. All edges are included. The two subdivisions are shown in Figure <a href="#fig:certificate-domains" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:certificate-domains">1</a>.

<figure id="fig:certificate-domains">

<figcaption>Subdivisions of the Weyl-norm domains $𝒟 = {(v,b) : v, b ≥ 0, v + b ≤ 2}$ and $𝒟_{+} = 𝒟 ∩ {v ≥ b}$ used in the triangular coefficient table. Every edge is included. The norm condition $v + b ≤ 2$ is a necessary relaxation of the sectional-curvature condition; the tensor estimates retain the stronger constraint $x + y ≤ 2$.</figcaption>
</figure>

Visual witness description of PDF page 43 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

Two side-by-side planar subdivision diagrams on $(v, b)$ coordinate axes:

- Triangle with boundary vertices $O(0,0)$, $U(2,0)$ on the $v$-axis, and $W_0(0,2)$ on the $b$-axis, with the hypotenuse labeled $v + b = 2$.
- Point $S(1,0)$ on the $v$-axis and point $C(1,1)$ in the interior.
- Internal line segments $OC$ and $SC$ partition the domain $\mathcal{D}$ into three triangles: $OSC$, $OCW_0$, and $SCU$.
Right diagram ($\mathcal{D}_+$: certificate $\mathcal{B}$):

- Intermediate vertices labeled $A(1/2, 1/2)$ on $OC$, $E(1, 1/2)$, and $B(3/2, 1/2)$ on $CU$.
- A horizontal line segment runs from $A$ through $E$ to $B$.
- A vertical line segment connects $C$, $E$, and $S$.

For a matrix row, the last two columns below bound, respectively, $$\min([r]_{ij},[h']_{ij}),\qquad
 [r]_{ij}[h']_{ij}-[s']_{ij}^{2}.$$ Thus the last column is the determinant of each coefficient matrix. For a scalar row, the first bound applies directly to its coefficient. The bounds apply to every nonzero coefficient; a zero matrix exception means that all three of its entries vanish.

<div class="center">

|                    |                   |      |       |          |            |
|:-------------------|:-----------------:|:----:|:-----:|---------:|-----------:|
| Object             |     Triangle      | $N$  | Zeros |          |            |
| scalar bound       | Determinant bound |      |       |          |            |
| $\mathcal A$       |       $OSC$       | $8$  |  $0$  |  $16000$ | $53000000$ |
|                    |      $OCW_0$      | $8$  |  $0$  |    $890$ |  $3900000$ |
|                    |       $SCU$       | $8$  |  $0$  |   $2400$ |  $3500000$ |
| $\mathcal B$       |       $OAS$       | $9$  |  $0$  |     $43$ |     $1050$ |
|                    |       $ASE$       | $9$  |  $0$  |     $43$ |      $490$ |
|                    |       $ACE$       | $10$ |  $1$  |      $2$ |       $21$ |
|                    |       $SBE$       | $9$  |  $0$  |     $43$ |     $1300$ |
|                    |       $CBE$       | $10$ |  $2$  | $13/100$ |        $4$ |
|                    |       $SUB$       | $9$  |  $0$  |      $5$ |      $290$ |
| $L_v$              |       $OCU$       | $6$  |  $1$  |   $9/10$ |          — |
| $F+12c\delta^2L_b$ |       $OSC$       | $9$  |  $6$  | $19/100$ |          — |
|                    |       $SCU$       | $9$  |  $7$  | $9/1000$ |          — |

</div>

Here are explicit expansion instructions for all entries of the table. They also fix the coefficient ordering. Write $a=V-1$, $s=W-1$ in <a href="#eq:15" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:15">[eq:15]</a> and form $\kappa(V,W)$ and its two partial derivatives. A partial derivative is obtained by reducing the appropriate exponent of each monomial and multiplying by that exponent. Differentiate before substituting the affine expressions $V,W$. Put $$\kappa_* =\kappa(V,W),\qquad \bar\kappa_* =\kappa(W,V).$$ Thus the partial derivatives of the exchanged function, before affine substitution, are $$\partial_V\bar\kappa_*(V,W)=(\partial_2\kappa)(W,V),\qquad
 \partial_W\bar\kappa_*(V,W)=(\partial_1\kappa)(W,V).$$

Ordinary coefficient arrays are multiplied by convolution: $$(PQ)_{de}=\sum_{f=0}^d\sum_{g=0}^e P_{fg}Q_{d-f,e-g}.$$ Use $$\begin{aligned}
 m_*&=\left(1-\frac38(V-W)\right)^2,
       &d_0&=2-V-W,\\
 P_{k,*}&=300m_*+160h d_0,
       &P_{l,*}&=35m_*V,\\
 Y_*&=m_*(42+3W^2+10V^2)
       -h\bigl[6(V^2+W^2-V-W)-26d_0\bigr].
\end{aligned}$$ For $\mathcal A$, apply $s_{ij}$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:bernstein-conversion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:bernstein-conversion">31</a> to the three entries of $$\begin{aligned}
 &(P_{k,*}+30hV)
       \bigl\{V(A_1,Y_*,A_{12})-\kappa_*(5,1,0)\bigr\}\\
 &\hspace{22mm}-2V^2(P_{k,*}^2,P_{l,*}^2,P_{k,*}P_{l,*}),
\end{aligned}$$ where $$A_1=5Y_*+m_*(300V+140V^2)+160h d_0V,
 \qquad
 A_{12}=m_*(75V+35V^2)+40h d_0V.$$ These are the original entries of <a href="#eq:21" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:21">[eq:21]</a> at $\sigma=1$, expressed only in the affine polynomials $V,W$.

For $\mathcal B$, put $$\begin{aligned}
 K_*&=4+V^2+W^2,\qquad \Theta_* =\Theta(V,W),\\
 X_*&=m_*(-70+100V-25V^2-5W^2)
       +\frac53h\bigl(6(a^2+s^2)+32a d_0\bigr),\\
 H_*&=8VK_*\Theta_*(1+V)\\
 &\quad-(1+6V+V^2)
       \left\{VD+K_*\left[V\bigl(X_*+(\partial_1\kappa)(V,W)\bigr)
                                     +\frac{2\kappa_*}{3}\right]\right\}.
\end{aligned}$$ Apply $s_{ij}$ to $H_*$, to its exchange in $V,W$, and to $$S_*(V,W)\left\{
       \frac{K_*}{2}\bigl((\partial_2\kappa)(V,W)
                        +(\partial_2\kappa)(W,V)\bigr)-D\right\}.$$ For example, on $CBE$ the triples at $(i,j)=(8,0),(8,1),(8,2)$ are $$\left(\frac{11041}{810},\frac{4063}{810},\frac{60557}{16200}\right),
 \quad
 \left(\frac{4328}{405},\frac{839}{405},\frac{3977}{2700}\right),
 \quad
 \left(\frac{191}{81},\frac{191}{81},-\frac{1189}{1350}\right).$$

For the two scalar rows, apply $s_{ij}$ to $10h d_0V-\kappa_*$ and, respectively, to $$\begin{aligned}
 c(V,W)\bigg\{&D\left(1-\frac{d_0^2}{4}\right)(V-W)^2
       +30h d_0(V^2a^2+W^2s^2)\\
 &+6(Va\kappa_*+Ws\bar\kappa_*)
       +12d_0^2(10h d_0W-\bar\kappa_*)\bigg\}-\Theta_*^2.
\end{aligned}$$ Together with the coefficient-sum formula, these prescriptions produce the displayed rational bounds by addition and multiplication of finite arrays.

The zero exceptions are as follows, with the third homogeneous index always $N-i-j$. For $\mathcal B$, they are the vertex $C$ in $ACE$ and $CBE$, namely $(0,10,0)$ and $(10,0,0)$, and also $(9,1,0)$ in $CBE$. For $L_v$ the only zero is $(0,6,0)$, the vertex $C$ in $OCU$. For $F+12c\delta^2L_b$ on $OSC$, the zeros are exactly $i+j\le2$. On $SCU$ they are exactly $9-j\le2$, together with $(i,j)=(0,6)$. There are no other exceptions.

Every nonzero matrix coefficient has positive diagonal entries and positive determinant, and each exceptional matrix is zero. Thus all coefficient matrices are positive semidefinite. The scalar coefficients are all nonnegative. Lemma <a href="#lem:bernstein-conversion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:bernstein-conversion">31</a> and the stated domain coverage prove the assertions. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
#### The remaining potential polynomial

<a id="stmt-lem-potential-certificate"></a>
<div id="lem:potential-certificate" class="lemma">

**Lemma 34** (The polynomial $F$). *The polynomial $F=cE_0-\Theta^2$ is nonnegative on $\mathcal D$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-potential-certificate"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-potential-certificate</summary>

*Proof.* Use $v=1-n+t$, $b=1-n-t$. The full domain becomes $$0\le n\le1,\qquad |t|\le1-n.$$ Write $F_*(n,t)=F(1-n+t,1-n-t)$ for the pullback to these coordinates. It is even in $t$, and direct expansion gives $F_*=\sum_{j=0}^4 f_j(n)t^{2j}$, where $$\begin{aligned}
 240f_0={}&n^3(1-n)\bigl(53511n^4-151593n^3
                 +222537n^2-83607n+10256\bigr),\\
 60f_1={}&n\bigl(19389n^5-418926n^4+523719n^3
                 -289776n^2+67782n+10564\bigr),\\
 60f_2={}&-1260129n^4+1370598n^3-451164n^2+23442n+74,\\
 10f_3={}&-12200n^2+2601n+75,\qquad
 f_4=-\frac{23}{5}.
\end{aligned}$$ For $n<1$, put $z=t^2/(1-n)^2\in[0,1]$. The degree-four Bernstein coefficients in $z$ are $$h_i(n)=\sum_{j=0}^i
       \frac{\binom ij}{\binom4j}f_j(n)(1-n)^{2j},
 \qquad 0\le i\le4.$$ Define $$d_0(n)=n^3(1-n),\qquad d_1(n)=n(1-n),\qquad
 d_2(n)=d_3(n)=d_4(n)=1-n.$$ Each quotient $q_i=h_i/d_i$ is a polynomial. Applying the interval conversion formula to it on each of the four intervals $$[0,1/4],\quad[1/4,1/2],\quad[1/2,3/4],\quad[3/4,1]$$ gives the following lower bounds for every coefficient:

<div class="center">

| $i$ | Divisor $d_i$ | Degree of $q_i$ | Lower bound |
|:---:|:-------------:|:---------------:|:-----------:|
| $0$ |  $n^3(1-n)$   |       $4$       |     $3$     |
| $1$ |   $n(1-n)$    |       $6$       |    $31$     |
| $2$ |     $1-n$     |       $7$       |    $1/5$    |
| $3$ |     $1-n$     |       $7$       |   $12/5$    |
| $4$ |     $1-n$     |       $7$       |    $9/5$    |

</div>

The complete prescription for each entry is to insert the displayed $f_j$ into $h_i$, cancel the indicated polynomial factor, and apply the coefficient sum in Lemma <a href="#lem:bernstein-conversion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:bernstein-conversion">31</a> in the listed degree. In particular, the identities $h_i=d_iq_i$ hold as polynomial identities, including at the endpoints.

Since every $d_i$ is nonnegative on $[0,1]$, every $h_i$ is nonnegative. Therefore $$F_*=\sum_{i=0}^4h_i(n)\binom4i z^i(1-z)^{4-i}\ge0
 \qquad(n<1).$$ At $n=0$, the coordinate $z=t^2$ still covers the entire permitted interval $|t|\le1$; the vanishing of $h_0,h_1$ causes no difficulty. At $n=1$, the domain contains only $t=0$, and directly $F_*(1,0)=F(0,0)=f_0(1)=0$. Thus no numerical division by a vanishing factor is required, and all of $\mathcal D$ is covered. ◻

</details>

Lemmas <a href="#lem:scalar-certificates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scalar-certificates">32</a>, <a href="#lem:matrix-certificates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:matrix-certificates">33</a>, and <a href="#lem:potential-certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:potential-certificate">34</a> establish every polynomial inequality used in Section <a href="#sec:coupling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:coupling">6</a>. Hence the three estimates of Proposition <a href="#prop:coupling-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:coupling-estimates">28</a> hold on the stated positive-variable spectral domain. The compatible limit in Section <a href="#sec:coupling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:coupling">6</a> extends them to the zero sets under the nonnegative sectional-curvature hypothesis.

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "Subdivisions of the Weyl-norm domains $𝒟 = {(v,b) : v, b ≥ 0, v + b ≤ 2}$ and $𝒟_{+} = 𝒟 ∩ {v ≥ b}$ used in the triangular coefficient table. Every edge is included. The norm condition $v + b ≤ 2$ is a necessary relaxation of the sectional-curvature condition; the tensor estimates retain the stronger constraint $x + y ≤ 2$.",
      "figure_id": "fig:certificate-domains",
      "pdf_page": 43,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:certificate-domains",
          "exact_quote": "Subdivisions of the Weyl-norm domains\n$\\mathcal D=\\{(v,b):v,b\\ge0,\\ v+b\\le2\\}$ and\n$\\mathcal D_+=\\mathcal D\\cap\\{v\\ge b\\}$ used in the triangular\ncoefficient table. Every edge is included. The norm condition\n$v+b\\le2$ is a necessary relaxat",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.7, every node/.style={font=\\small}]\n \\begin{scope}\n  \\fill[blue!4] (0,0)--(2,0)--(0,2)--cycle;\n  \\draw[->,black!55] (0,0)--(2.25,0) node[right] {$v$};\n  \\draw[->,black!55] (0,0)--(0,2.25) node[above] {$b$};\n  \\draw[thick] (0,0)--(2,0)--(0,2)--cycle;\n  \\path (2,0)--(0,2) node[pos=.76,above,sloped,font=\\scriptsize]\n    {$v+b=2$};\n  \\draw (0,0)--(1,1)--(1,0);\n  \\node[below left] at (0,0) {$O$};\n  \\node[below] at (1,0) {$S$};\n  \\node[below right] at (2,0) {$U$};\n  \\node[above right] at (1,1) {$C$};\n  \\node[above left] at (0,2) {$W_0$};\n  \\node at (1,-0.48) {$\\mathcal D$: certificate $\\mathcal A$};\n \\end{scope}\n \\begin{scope}[xshift=3.6cm]\n  \\fill[blue!4] (0,0)--(2,0)--(1,1)--cycle;\n  \\draw[->,black!55] (0,0)--(2.25,0) node[right] {$v$};\n  \\draw[->,black!55] (0,0)--(0,1.3) node[above] {$b$};\n  \\draw[thick] (0,0)--(2,0)--(1,1)--cycle;\n  \\path (0,0)--(1,1) node[pos=.73,above,sloped,font=\\scriptsize]\n    {$v=b$};\n  \\draw (0.5,0.5)--(1,0)--(1.5,0.5);\n  \\draw (1,0)--(1,1);\n  \\draw (0.5,0.5)--(1.5,0.5);\n  \\node[below left] at (0,0) {$O$};\n  \\node[below] at (1,0) {$S$};\n  \\node[below right] at (2,0) {$U$};\n  \\node[above] at (1,1) {$C$};\n  \\node[above left] at (0.5,0.5) {$A$};\n  \\node[above right] at (1.5,0.5) {$B$};\n  \\node[above right,inner sep=2pt] at (1,0.5) {$E$};\n  \\node at (1,-0.48) {$\\mathcal D_+$: certificate $\\mathcal B$};\n \\end{scope}\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Two side-by-side planar subdivision diagrams on $(v, b)$ coordinate axes:\nLeft diagram ($\\mathcal{D}$: certificate $\\mathcal{A}$):\n- Horizontal axis labeled $v$, vertical axis labeled $b$.\n- Triangle with boundary vertices $O(0,0)$, $U(2,0)$ on the $v$-axis, and $W_0(0,2)$ on the $b$-axis, with the hypotenuse labeled $v + b = 2$.\n- Point $S(1,0)$ on the $v$-axis and point $C(1,1)$ in the interior.\n- Internal line segments $OC$ and $SC$ partition the domain $\\mathcal{D}$ into three triangles: $OSC$, $OCW_0$, and $SCU$.\nRight diagram ($\\mathcal{D}_+$: certificate $\\mathcal{B}$):\n- Horizontal axis labeled $v$, vertical axis labeled $b$.\n- Triangular domain $\\mathcal{D}_+$ bounded by segment $OC$ along the line labeled $v = b$, segment $CU$, and segment $OU$ along the $v$-axis.\n- Intermediate vertices labeled $A(1/2, 1/2)$ on $OC$, $E(1, 1/2)$, and $B(3/2, 1/2)$ on $CU$.\n- A horizontal line segment runs from $A$ through $E$ to $B$.\n- A vertical line segment connects $C$, $E$, and $S$.\n- Oblique line segments connect $S$ to $A$ and $S$ to $B$.\n- The domain is partitioned into six triangles: $OAS$, $ASE$, $ACE$, $SBE$, $CBE$, and $SUB$."
    }
  ],
